\title{Linnik's constant below the zero-density exponent: $21/10$ from a zero-free half-plane}
\author{Ritchie Comley}
\date{Draft, 16 November 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We prove that for some constant $K$ every reduced residue class modulo $q$ contains a prime $p \le K q^{21/10}$.
The proof uses two recent results that have not been refereed: OpenAI's zero-free half-plane
$\operatorname{Re} s > 11/12$ for Dirichlet $L$-functions, and Chen, Gupta and Li's large values estimate for
Dirichlet polynomials with characters. With the half-plane and Huxley's zero-density estimate alone we prove the
exponent $43/20$. Both theorems also count the primes: for $x$ larger than a constant times $q^L$, $L$ the exponent,
the residue class contains $\gg x/(\varphi(q) \log x)$ primes $p \le x$.

These exponents are not to be compared with Xylouris's $5$, whose proof uses refereed results only. From the
half-plane, the classical argument gives every exponent above $12/5$ with Huxley's estimate and above $7/3$ with Chen,
Gupta and Li's density estimate. A theorem of Harman on primes in a progression gives $1/0.472 < 2.1187$ from the
half-plane; our $43/20$ is larger, and our $21/10$ smaller. Like Harman's, our proofs use his sieve, fed here by a
Type~II estimate that sorts the characters by the largest real part of the zeros of their $L$-functions; a finite
computation bounds the fraction of the primes that the sieve loses.

The whole proof is formalised in Lean~4 with Mathlib, including both recent results. The proof at $43/20$ depends
only on Lean's standard axioms. So does the proof at $21/10$ once the check of its finite computation by Lean's
kernel ends without error; that check was not finished at the time of writing.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

Linnik's theorem bounds the least prime in a residue class. There are constants $K$ and $L$ such that every reduced
residue class mod $q$ contains a prime $p \le Kq^L$. We prove it with $L = 21/10$, and with $L = 43/20$ from fewer
inputs. Let $\pi(x; q, a)$ be the number of primes $p \le x$ with $p \equiv a \pmod q$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:2110}
% Lean: Linnik21000.linnik_21000
There is $K$ such that for every $q \ge 1$ and every $a$ prime to $q$ there is a prime $p \equiv a \pmod q$ with
$p \le K q^{21/10}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:4320}
% Lean: Linnik21500.linnik_21500
There is $K$ such that for every $q \ge 1$ and every $a$ prime to $q$ there is a prime $p \equiv a \pmod q$ with
$p \le K q^{43/20}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:count}
% Lean: Linnik21000.count_21000, Linnik21000.count_large_21000, Linnik21500.count_21500, Linnik21500.count_large_21500
Let $L = 21/10$ or $L = 43/20$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] There are $c > 0$ and $K$ such that for every $q \ge 1$, every $a$ prime to $q$ and every $x \ge Kq^L$,
  \[
    \pi(x; q, a) \ge c\, \frac{x}{\varphi(q) \log x}.
  \]
\item[(b)] For large $q$ and every $a$ prime to $q$, the primes $p \equiv a \pmod q$ with $q^L < p \le 2q^L$ number
  at least
  \[
    \frac{1 - C}2 \cdot \frac{q^L}{\varphi(q) \log q^L},
  \]
  where $C < 1$ is the cost of the certificate at the exponent $L$, divided by $2^{60}$
  (Propositions~\ref{prop:certificate} and~\ref{prop:certificate-b}): $C = 0.97716\ldots$ at $21/10$ and
  $C = 0.94846\ldots$ at $43/20$, so that $(1 - C)/2 > 0.0114$ at $21/10$ and $(1 - C)/2 > 0.0257$ at $43/20$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} is the weaker statement, stated because its proof uses less: the zero-free half-plane of
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, the one result it uses that has not been refereed, and Huxley's density estimate. Its
exponent is not the smallest that the half-plane gives: with the same half-plane, Harman's theorem on primes in a
progression \cite{harman05} gives $1/0.472 < 2.1187$ (see the end of the next subsection).
Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} uses a second such result, the large values estimate of Chen, Gupta and Li (Input~\ref{inp:cgl}),
and three further rules in the sieve (items 6 and 7 below). The corollary is proved with the theorems
(Sections~\ref{sec:proof} and~\ref{sec:certificate-b}) and uses no more than they do. The certificate at $21/10$
records the finite computation on which Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} and the corollary at $21/10$ rest; its check by Lean's
kernel was not finished at the time of writing (Section~\ref{sec:lean}). Sections~\ref{sec:inputs}
to~\ref{sec:proof} prove Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}, and Sections~\ref{sec:window} to~\ref{sec:certificate-b} add what
Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} needs.

\subsection*{Earlier exponents}

Linnik \cite{linnik44, linnik44b} proved the theorem with absolute constants $K$ and $L$ (see
\cite[Chapter~18]{iwaniec-kowalski} for an account). The admissible exponent $L$, Linnik's constant, has been lowered
many times: Heath-Brown \cite{heath-brown92} reached $L = 5.5$, and Xylouris $L = 5.18$ \cite{xylouris-acta} and then
$L = 5$ \cite{xylouris11}, and again $L = 5$ in a journal article \cite{xylouris18}.
Under the generalised Riemann hypothesis every $L > 2$
is admissible, classically by the explicit formula, and Lamzouri, Li and Soundararajan give an explicit bound of the
order of $(\varphi(q) \log q)^2$ \cite{lls, lls-corr}. Naslund \cite{naslund} has announced $L = 3.99$ for large $q$,
in a preprint that has not been refereed; his proof builds on the method of Heath-Brown and Xylouris and uses neither
of the two inputs of this paper.

Theorems~\ref{thm:2110} and~\ref{thm:4320} have $L = 2.1$ and $L = 2.15$, but they are not on the same footing as
Heath-Brown's and Xylouris's exponents. Those proofs use refereed results only; the proofs of Theorems~\ref{thm:2110}
and~\ref{thm:4320} use recent results that have not been refereed (Section~\ref{sec:inputs}). With the zero-free
half-plane and Huxley's density estimate the classical argument already gives every $L > 12/5$, and with Chen, Gupta
and Li's density estimate every $L > 7/3$. The value $2.4$ is recorded in Tao's collaborative list of constants
\cite{tao-constants} as a deduction, not refereed, from the half-plane $\operatorname{Re} s > 7/8$ of OpenAI's main
preprint (Section~\ref{sec:inputs}) and the classical density estimate. Against the classical argument,
Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} goes from $12/5$ to $43/20$ and Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} from $7/3$ to $21/10$.

The half-plane gives more than the classical argument through Harman's theorems on primes in a progression
\cite{harman05, harman08}, which assume a zero-free region for the modulus that Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} supplies
(see the subsection on related work below). His theorem of 2005 then gives Linnik's theorem with the exponent
$1/0.472 < 2.1187$, and his theorem of 2008 gives $1/0.4736 < 2.1115$ for large moduli with only small prime
factors. So the exponent of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} is not the smallest that the half-plane gives: it is larger than
both of these, and that of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} is smaller than both.

\subsection*{The zeros near $\operatorname{Re} s = 1$}

The difficulty in Linnik's theorem is the zeros of the $L$-functions mod $q$ near the line $\operatorname{Re} s = 1$.
By the explicit formula, a zero $\beta + i\gamma$ of $L(s, \chi)$ contributes about $x^{\beta - 1}$, relative to the
main term, to the count of primes $p \le x$ in a residue class mod $q$. A zero-density estimate with exponent $A$, a
bound of about $(qT)^{A(1 - \sigma)}$ for the number of zeros with real part at least $\sigma$, makes the zeros of
real part about $\sigma$ contribute about $(q^A/x)^{1 - \sigma}$ together. Classically the zero-free region allows
$1 - \beta$ as small as $c/\log q$, and one exceptional real zero may be closer still; at $x = q^L$ such a zero
contributes a constant, not a small quantity. The record proofs control these zeros with zero-free regions, density
estimates without logarithmic factors and the Deuring--Heilbronn phenomenon, and the constants of all three enter the
exponent.

The zero-free half-plane of OpenAI's release \cite{openai-main, openai-companion}, Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, removes
the zeros with $\beta > 11/12$. Every remaining zero contributes at most a power $x^{-1/12}$, and the problem becomes
one of density: a density exponent $A \ge 2$ for $1/2 \le \sigma \le 11/12$ gives Linnik's theorem with every exponent
$L > A$ (Section~\ref{sec:comparison}). Huxley \cite{huxley75} gives $A = 12/5$. Chen, Gupta and Li \cite{cgl}, by Guth
and Maynard's method \cite{guth-maynard}, prove $A = 7/3$, uniformly in $q$ and $T$ (their Theorem~1.2);
Figure~\ref{fig:density} shows these exponents. The classical deduction uses that there are no zeros near
$\operatorname{Re} s = 1$, and in particular no exceptional zero; the sieve of this paper uses the same.
Input~\ref{inp:cgl} is Chen, Gupta and Li's large values estimate (their Proposition~3.1), from which, with Ingham's
and Huxley's estimates, the paper's Lean library (Section~\ref{sec:lean}) deduces a density exponent of at most
$11667/5000$.

\subsection*{Harman's sieve, and why it can go below the density exponent}

Harman's sieve \cite{harman83}, \cite[Chapter~3]{harman} bounds from below the number of primes in the set $\mathcal A$
of the $n \in (x, 2x]$ with $n \equiv a \pmod q$, by comparing $\mathcal A$ with the set $\mathcal B$ of the $n \in (x,
2x]$ prime to $q$, weighted by $1/\varphi(q)$. Type~I information is this comparison on the multiples of an integer
prime to $q$; counting in residue classes gives it. Type~II information is the comparison on sums with bounded
coefficients over $n = n_1 n_2 r$, with $n_1$ and $n_2$ of given lengths and $r$ a product of primes, the \emph{rest}; a
Type~II estimate gives it. The sieve decomposes the count of primes in $\mathcal A$ by Buchstab's identity into terms of
the sign $+$ or $-$, which need a lower or an upper bound. It compares with $\mathcal B$ the terms that it can, and
drops others of the sign $+$, which keeps a lower bound. A finite computation bounds the fraction of the primes that it
loses.

For primes in short intervals a density exponent $A$ gives intervals of length $x^{1 - 1/A}$, and Harman's sieve goes
further, to $x^{0.525}$ \cite{bhp}. The same happens for primes in a progression: Harman showed it in his work on
Carmichael numbers \cite{harman05, harman08}, where his sieve reaches the moduli $q < x^{0.472}$, beyond the
$x^{5/12}$ of the density exponent $12/5$; and it happens here. The Type~II estimate bounds
\[
  \sum_{\chi \ne \chi_0} |W(\chi) S(\chi)|,
\]
where $W$ is a Dirichlet polynomial with bounded coefficients, or a product of two, and $S$ a product of \emph{prime
sums}, sums of $\chi(p)$ over the primes of a range. Sort the characters mod $q$ into \emph{bins} by the largest real
part $\beta$ of the zeros of their $L$-functions. In the bin of $\beta$ each prime sum of length $Q$ is at most about
$Q^\beta$, and a density estimate with exponent $A$ bounds the bin's size by $q^{A(1 - \beta)}$.

In one variable, with one polynomial $W$ of length $P = q^m$ and $S$ of length $x/P$, Cauchy--Schwarz over a bin and
orthogonality bound $\sum |W(\chi)|$ by the square root of the bin's size times $((q + P)P)^{1/2}$. Neglecting
logarithms and margins, the bound is smaller than the main term for $P$ around $q$ exactly when $L$ exceeds $1 + A/2$,
with $A$ the largest density exponent over $\sigma$: $11/5$ with Huxley's $12/5$, and $13/6$ with Chen, Gupta and Li's
$7/3$.

In two variables, with two polynomials $W_1$ and $W_2$ of lengths $q^{m_1}$ and $q^{m_2}$, and prime sums over the
primes of the rest, of total length $q^\rho$ with $\rho = L - m_1 - m_2$, Cauchy--Schwarz is taken between $W_1$ and
$W_2$ instead. Each mean square over
a bin is then at most $(q + q^{m_i})$ times the sum of the squared coefficients, whatever the bin's size. With $s_i = 1
- m_i$ and $s^+ = \max(s, 0)$, the bin of $\beta$ contributes, against the main term, about
\[
  q^{-\rho(1 - \beta) + (s_1^+ + s_2^+)/2}.
\]

For two polynomials of length at least $q$ this is small at every $\beta$ below the half-plane, with no density
estimate. A polynomial shorter than $q$ costs $q^{s_i/2}$, which the zeros' saving $q^{-\rho(1 - \beta)}$ must cover,
and that saving vanishes as $\beta$ approaches $1$. There the bins are small, and the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality
in mean square, with Huxley's density estimate, takes over from orthogonality. It does so from $\beta = 0.7789$ on, just
above the point $0.7784$ from which, with Huxley's estimate, it beats orthogonality on a polynomial of length about
$q$; below $0.7789$ the estimate uses no density estimate (Section~\ref{sec:region}).
So the estimate holds on an explicit region of the two lengths for every $L$ (Section~\ref{sec:type2}), and the
sieve's task is to bring as many of its terms as it can into that region.

When $S$ is a product of a few prime sums, the estimate in one variable can also go below $1 + A/2$. What it needs is
not the number of characters with a zero but the number at which the prime sums are large, and the prime sums are
polynomials of known lengths. The $k$-th power of a prime sum of length $Q$ has length about $Q^k$ and bounded
coefficients, so by orthogonality the sum is at least $Q^u$ at no more than about $\max(q, Q^k)\, Q^{k(1 - 2u)}$
characters. For $Q^k$ just above $q$ this is $q^{2(1 - u)}$, the count that a density exponent $2$ would give.
Input~\ref{inp:cgl}, applied to the same power, gives a second count. So the characters of a bin are sorted further,
into \emph{classes}, by the size of each prime sum, and each class is bounded by orthogonality or by the
Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality. The estimate holds at the lengths at which the bound for every class is below the main
term; the estimate and its tests are called the \emph{resonant window} (Section~\ref{sec:window}).

The sieve cannot bring all its terms within reach of these estimates, and it pays for the terms that it cannot
compare. Of the
expected number $q^L/(\varphi(q) \log q^L)$ of primes in $(q^L, 2q^L]$ it loses at most the fraction $C$ of
Corollary~\ref{cor:count}(b): $C < 0.94847$ at $L = 43/20$ (Section~\ref{sec:certificate}), and at $L = 21/10$, with the
further rules of Sections~\ref{sec:window} and~\ref{sec:rules}, $C < 0.97717$ (Section~\ref{sec:certificate-b}).
Corollary~\ref{cor:count}(b) counts at least half of what remains. Table~\ref{tab:exponents} compares what the inputs
give with and without the sieve of this paper.

\begin{table}[t]

Chen, Gupta and Li's density estimate would not improve Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}: above $0.7789$, the only place where
its estimate in two variables uses a density estimate, Huxley's exponent is the smaller of the two
(Section~\ref{sec:region}).
Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} uses Input~\ref{inp:cgl} twice: in the counts of the estimate in one variable, and in the
estimate of item~7 below (Section~\ref{sec:leaf-estimate}), which counts the characters with a zero below $0.7789$ as
well, by Ingham's estimate and Input~\ref{inp:cgl}.

\subsection*{What is new}

The paper's contributions are eight. Items 1 to 4 prove Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}; Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} uses items 1
to 3, the search of item 4, and items 5 to 8.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{A Type~II estimate in two variables over the zeros' real parts} (Section~\ref{sec:type2}). In the
  Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality in mean square the bin's size multiplies only the bound for the off-diagonal terms.
  The estimate holds on the region of Definition~\ref{def:region}, for every exponent.
\item \emph{Harman's comparison in two variables} (Section~\ref{sec:type1}). Steps of Buchstab's identity bring a
  product of some of a term's primes into a range that the region of item~1 allows for $n_1$; the rest is a product
  of other primes, and $n_2$ is what remains of the integer. The branches that the steps do not stop, the
  \emph{leaves}, are compared by Type~I information, with a bilinear Type~I estimate that reaches moduli beyond
  $x/q$.
\item \emph{A decomposition by trees of Buchstab steps, with a reversal} (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}). A tree splits the
  sieve's terms by the ranges of their primes, evaluates a term where the estimate of item~1 or the
  comparison of item~2 applies, and drops others at a cost bounded by counts of rough numbers. A term of the sign $-$
  may be reversed: counted through its largest prime, as a term of a sieve on the quotients of its integers by their
  other primes and cofactor.
\item \emph{A certificate} (Section~\ref{sec:certificate}). A search outside Lean's kernel finds the trees; the kernel
  follows the plan that the search kept and checks the result by evaluation. The certificate is that plan, written down
  for the kernel, with the propositions about integers that the kernel checks. At $43/20$ the sieve loses less than
  $0.94847$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate}).
\item \emph{A Type~II estimate in one variable over the sizes of the prime sums} (Section~\ref{sec:window-estimate}).
  The classes of a bin are counted by the moments of the prime sums and by Input~\ref{inp:cgl} for their powers, and
  the estimate holds where the bound for every class is below the main term.
\item \emph{The tests of that estimate} (Sections~\ref{sec:window-terms} to~\ref{sec:window-tests}). A term is
  evaluated by the estimate in one variable: some of its primes, the rest, give the prime sums of $S$, and its other
  primes and its cofactor give $W$; a cofactor of one prime may be in the rest. The condition on the classes
  is checked on finitely many integers for a whole range of primes.
\item \emph{Two more rules} (Section~\ref{sec:rules}). Leaves beyond the reach of the bilinear Type~I estimate are cut
  in three ranges: two that it reaches, and a third bounded by an estimate in two variables with a fourth moment. A term
  of the sign $+$ may be reversed as in item~3.
\item \emph{The certificate at $21/10$} (Section~\ref{sec:certificate-b}). With items 5 to 7, the sieve loses less
  than $0.97717$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate-b}).
\end{enumerate}

The other ingredients are not new. Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} is OpenAI's and Input~\ref{inp:cgl} is Chen, Gupta and
Li's, by Guth and Maynard's method. The density estimates of Ingham \cite{ingham} and Huxley \cite{huxley72,
huxley75}, the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality \cite{halasz, montgomery69}, Buchstab's identity \cite{buchstab},
Brun's sieve \cite{brun} and Harman's sieve are classical, and role reversal is taken from \cite{bhp} and
\cite{harman}. The bilinear Type~I estimate is proved by orthogonality, Cauchy--Schwarz and Parseval's identity
over the inverses mod $q$, with no bound for Kloosterman sums. Sorting a sieve's own polynomials by their sizes and
counting them by the mean values of their powers is the practice of the sieves for primes in short intervals
\cite{hb-iwaniec, bhp, harman}.

\subsection*{Related work}

Heath-Brown and Iwaniec \cite{hb-iwaniec} combined a sieve with zero-density estimates, which bound Dirichlet
polynomials over primes in the $t$-aspect; Section~\ref{sec:type2} applies the idea in the $q$-aspect, sorting the
characters mod $q$ into bins by their zeros. Harman's sieve with a numerical bound for its loss is Harman's method
\cite{harman}, as in Baker, Harman and Pintz \cite{bhp} and R.~Li \cite{li-harman}.

Harman himself applied it to primes in a progression to one large modulus, in his work on Carmichael numbers
\cite{harman05, harman08}. Alford, Granville and Pomerance \cite{agp} had the ranges $q \le x^B$ for $B < 5/12$
there, and Harman raised $5/12$ to $0.472$. His Theorem~2 in \cite{harman05} gives
$\pi(x; q, a) \gg x/(\varphi(q) \log x)$ whenever $q < x^{0.472}$, for every large modulus $q$ that satisfies a
condition on the zeros: the $L$-functions of the primitive characters to the divisors of $q$ have no zero in a region
of width $(\log q)^{-3/4}$ to the left of the line $\operatorname{Re} s = 1$, up to a height that grows with $q$. His
Theorem~1.2 in \cite{harman08} has the range $q < x^{0.4736}$ under that condition and a condition on the prime
factors: all prime factors of $q$ are below $q^\delta$, for a certain $\delta > 0$. Section~\ref{sec:comparison}
quotes the statements. R.~Li's Theorem~9 \cite{li-harman} has the same two conditions and the range
$q < x^{0.4752}$; for the powers of a fixed prime he deduces the exponent $1/0.4752 < 2.1044$ (his Theorem~11).

Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} implies the condition on the zeros for every large $q$. This is how Harman's theorems give
the exponents stated above, and R.~Li's Theorem~9 gives $1/0.4752$ for the moduli that satisfy the condition on the
prime factors, if it holds as stated (Section~\ref{sec:comparison}).

\subsection*{Formalisation}

The Lean statements of Theorems~\ref{thm:2110} and~\ref{thm:4320} have no hypotheses, and their proofs use no axiom
beyond Lean's three standard ones (Section~\ref{sec:lean}), for Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} once the check of its
certificate (above) ends without error. The library proves the two inputs and the density estimates of Ingham and
Huxley itself; so the truth of the theorems rests on Lean's kernel, Mathlib and the toolchain, not on the preprints.
Whether the Lean statement of an input captures the preprint's theorem is not checked by the kernel; it matters for
attributing and interpreting the input, not for the truth of the theorems. Section~\ref{sec:inputs} compares each
input with its source, and Section~\ref{sec:lean} says what is known of independent checking of the inputs.

Every step of the proofs, and every result from the literature that they use, is proved in the library \cite{repo}
(Lean~4 \cite{lean4} with Mathlib \cite{mathlib}) or in Mathlib, at $21/10$ subject to the same check.
Appendix~\ref{app:claims} names the Lean theorem behind each claim and Section~\ref{sec:lean} says what is formalised;
the proofs of the paper are those of the library, at the level of detail of a paper, except where
Section~\ref{sec:inputs} says otherwise, at Propositions~\ref{prop:curve} and~\ref{prop:curve-star}. Where the Lean
proof fixes a constant whose value the argument does not need, the paper usually says only that the constant exists.
The argument, the formalisation and this text were developed with the help of AI systems (see the acknowledgements);
the author is responsible for the paper.

\subsection*{Outline}

The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Section~\ref{sec:inputs}: the inputs; counts of characters with a zero (Proposition~\ref{prop:curve};
  Proposition~\ref{prop:curve-star} serves Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} only).
\item Section~\ref{sec:type2}: the Type~II estimate in two variables (Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}).
\item Section~\ref{sec:type1}: Type~I estimates; Harman's comparison in two variables
  (Propositions~\ref{prop:harman-two} and~\ref{prop:harman-fill}).
\item Section~\ref{sec:decomp}: the trees; Linnik's theorem from a tree that bounds the sieve's loss below $1$
  (Theorem~\ref{thm:tree}); the step from $x = q^L$ to every $x$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:every-x}).
\item Section~\ref{sec:certificate}: the search; the certificate at $43/20$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate}).
\item Section~\ref{sec:proof}: the proofs of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} and of Corollary~\ref{cor:count} at $43/20$.
\end{itemize}
The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110}, on top of these:
\begin{itemize}
\item Section~\ref{sec:window}: the Type~II estimate in one variable (Theorem~\ref{thm:window}); its tests.
\item Section~\ref{sec:rules}: the estimate with a fourth moment (Theorem~\ref{thm:type2-leaves}); the leaves cut in
  three; the reversal of a term of the sign $+$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:reversal-plus}).
\item Section~\ref{sec:certificate-b}: the certificate at $21/10$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate-b}); the proofs
  of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} and of Corollary~\ref{cor:count} at $21/10$.
\end{itemize}
Section~\ref{sec:lean} describes the formalisation. Section~\ref{sec:comparison} compares the theorems with what the
inputs give without the sieve and with Harman's theorems, and discusses the method's limits. Appendix~\ref{app:claims}
is the claims table, and Appendix~\ref{app:terms} lists the paper's terms and notation. A supplement holds the proofs
that are standard or long, and the details of the formalisation and of the computations; its opening lists its
sections, which the paper cites as \S S1 to \S S8.

\section{Notation and the inputs}\label{sec:inputs}

Throughout, $q \ge 1$ is the modulus and $a$ is prime to $q$. The Dirichlet characters mod $q$ are $\chi$, the trivial
one is $\chi_0$, and $L(s, \chi)$ is the $L$-function of $\chi$, continued to the whole plane. For
$1/2 \le \sigma \le 1$ and $T \ge 2$ let
\[
  N^*(\sigma, T; q) = \#\{\chi \bmod q : L(s, \chi) = 0 \text{ for some } s,\
  \operatorname{Re} s \ge \sigma,\ |\operatorname{Im} s| \le T\},
\]
the number of characters mod $q$ whose $L$-function has a zero in that rectangle. It counts characters, not zeros, so
a bound
for the number of zeros bounds it. The proof is a sieve on the integers of $(x, 2x]$, $x = q^L$, with $L = 43/20$ or
$L = 21/10$ in the end, and $q$ is taken large. Appendix~\ref{app:terms} lists the paper's terms and notation, with
the place of each definition.

The paper states two inputs. A statement below that holds `under' an input is conditional in the text of the paper.
The library proves both inputs (Section~\ref{sec:lean}), so its theorems carry no such hypothesis; hence the word
\emph{Input} and not \emph{hypothesis}.
Sections~\ref{sec:type2} to~\ref{sec:proof} use the first input only.

\begin{inputthm}[the zero-free half-plane]\label{inp:half-plane}
% Lean: Eis.LFunction_ne_zero_of_re_gt
For every $q \ge 1$, every Dirichlet character $\chi$ mod $q$ and every $s$ with $\operatorname{Re} s > 11/12$,
$L(s, \chi) \ne 0$, except at $s = 1$ for the trivial character.
\end{inputthm}

The exception is the pole of $L(s, \chi_0)$ at $s = 1$. This is OpenAI's result: their companion preprint
\cite{openai-companion} proves it, and their main preprint \cite{openai-main} proves the stronger
$\operatorname{Re} s > 7/8$. The library proves it along the companion preprint's route (\S S5).

\begin{inputthm}[Chen, Gupta and Li's large values estimate]\label{inp:cgl}
% Lean: LinnikCGLLV.CGLProp31 (the statement), LinnikCGLHB101.prop31 (its proof)
For every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $C \ge 0$ such that the following holds. Let $q \ge 1$, $T \ge 1$ and
$7/10 \le \sigma \le 4/5$, and let $N$ be a real number with $N \ge (qT)^{2/3}/2$. Let $S$ be a finite set of integers
in $[6N/5, 9N/5]$, with coefficients $b_n \in \mathbb C$, $|b_n| \le 1$. Let $\chi_1, \dots, \chi_R$ be distinct
primitive characters mod $q$, and $t_1, \dots, t_R$ real numbers with $|t_r| \le T$ and
\[
  \Bigl| \sum_{n \in S} b_n \chi_r(n) n^{it_r} \Bigr| \ge \frac{N^\sigma}{6} \qquad (1 \le r \le R).
\]
Then
\[
  R \le C (qTN)^{\varepsilon} \bigl( N^{2 - 2\sigma} + (qT)^{1/2} N^{3 - 4\sigma} + (qT)^{4/3} N^{2 - 4\sigma} \bigr).
\]
\end{inputthm}

This is Proposition~3.1 of Chen, Gupta and Li \cite{cgl}, with one point for each character; their method is Guth and
Maynard's \cite{guth-maynard}. Their proposition bounds, up to a factor $C(\varepsilon)(qT)^\varepsilon$ for $qT$
large, a set of pairs $(t, \chi)$ whose pairs with the same character are $(qT)^\varepsilon$ apart. Its sum is $\sum_n
w(n/N) b_n \chi(n) n^{it}$, with the smooth weight $w$ of their \S 3, supported in $[1, 2]$ and equal to $1$ on $[6/5,
9/5]$, and its conditions are those of Input~\ref{inp:cgl}: $7/10 \le \sigma \le 4/5$, $N \ge (qT)^{2/3}/2$ and the
sum at least $N^\sigma/6$ in absolute value. Input~\ref{inp:cgl} takes distinct characters with one point each, the
coefficients $b_n \mathbf 1_S(n)$, on which the weight is $1$, and the factor $C(qTN)^\varepsilon$ for every $q$ and
$T$. The library proves Input~\ref{inp:cgl} along their proof (\S S5). One of its steps, their Theorem~10.1, is the
case of one modulus, for primitive characters, of Heath-Brown's large values estimate for double sums with characters
\cite[Theorem~1]{hb79b}, which they quote; the library proves that case with a factor $(qTM)^\varepsilon$ in place of
Heath-Brown's $D^{c_0/\log\log D}$, along Kerr's proof of the case $q = 1$ \cite{kerr}, with Jutila's Lemma~1
\cite{jutila} for the reflection with characters. Heath-Brown's theorem is refereed; Chen, Gupta and Li's preprint is
not. The formalisation found seven places where the preprint's text needs a small repair, none of which affects its
results (\S S5 and \file{formalisations/chen-gupta-li-density.md}).

Input~\ref{inp:cgl} is stated for primitive characters. A character mod $q$ is induced by a primitive character mod a
divisor $d$ of $q$, and a sum with the character is the sum with the primitive character and the coefficients
multiplied by $\mathbf 1_{(n, q) = 1}$.
The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} uses Input~\ref{inp:cgl} in the following form, for all the characters mod $q$;
the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} does not use it. For every $0 < \varepsilon \le 1$ there is $C \ge 0$ such that,
for $q \ge 1$, $T \ge 1$, an integer $N \ge (qT)^{2/3}$, a size $N^{7/10} \le V \le N^{4/5}$ and coefficients
$|a_n| \le 1$ on $(N, 2N]$, the number of characters mod $q$, primitive or not, with
$|\sum_{N < n \le 2N} a_n \chi(n) n^{it_\chi}| \ge V$ at a point $|t_\chi| \le T$ is at most
\[
  C q^\varepsilon (qTN)^\varepsilon \bigl(N^2 V^{-2} + (qT)^{1/2} N^3 V^{-4} + (qT)^{4/3} N^2 V^{-4}\bigr). \tag{CGL}
\]
Indeed, of the three parts of the sum, cut at $6N/5$ and $9N/5$, one is at least $V/3$. Apply Input~\ref{inp:cgl} to
the three parts at the lengths $cN$, $c = 11/15$, $1$ and $19/15$, each at least $(qT)^{2/3}/2$; with $V = N^\sigma$,
$7/10 \le \sigma \le 4/5$, the threshold $(cN)^\sigma/6$ is at most $V/3$ since $c^\sigma \le 2$. Then sum over the
divisors of $q$, of which there are at most $C(\varepsilon) q^\varepsilon$.

\subsection*{The density curve}

The Type~II estimate of Section~\ref{sec:type2} counts the characters by a curve.

\begin{definition}\label{def:curve}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIGen.CurveCounts
A function $g : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$ is a \emph{density curve} if there is $C \ge 0$ such that for every
$q \ge 1$, every $1/2 \le \sigma \le 1$ and every $T \ge 2$,
\[
  N^*(\sigma, T; q) \le C\, (qT)^{g(\sigma)} \bigl(1 + \log qT\bigr)^{10}.
\]
\end{definition}

A density estimate $N^*(\sigma, T; q) \ll (qT)^{A(1 - \sigma)}$, with $A$ constant and no factor $(qT)^\varepsilon$,
gives the curve $A(1 - \sigma)$, and the curve $1$ is the trivial count $N^*(\sigma, T; q) \le \varphi(q) \le qT$.

The Type~II estimate uses the curve only from the threshold $\beta^\dagger = 7789/10000$ on, where the
Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality takes over from orthogonality (Section~\ref{sec:region}). Below $\beta^\dagger$ it
bounds both polynomials by orthogonality, whatever the curve, so the trivial count there costs nothing. From
$\beta^\dagger$ on it checks the zone condition (Section~\ref{sec:zone}) piece by piece, on pieces where the curve is
$A(1 - \sigma)$ with $A$ constant. The curve of this paper is the trivial
count below $\beta^\dagger$ and Huxley's exponent $3/(3\sigma - 1)$ above, held constant on short steps and rounded up,
with a
constant exponent from $5/6$ on, where Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} removes the zeros beyond $11/12$. The curve
$g_{\mathrm H}$ is the following.
\begin{itemize}
\item $g_{\mathrm H}(\sigma) = 1$ for $\sigma < 7789/10000$.
\item $g_{\mathrm H}(\sigma) = (7014/3125)(1 - \sigma)$ on $[7789/10000, 131/168)$, an interval of length less than
  $10^{-3}$ ($131/168 = 0.77976\ldots$): Huxley's exponent at the left end
  is $30000/13367 = 2.244333\ldots$, and $7014/3125 = 2.24448$.
\item On $[131/168, 5/6)$, divided into fifteen steps $[\sigma_-, \sigma_+)$ of length $1/280$,
  $g_{\mathrm H}(\sigma) = \bigl(3/(3\sigma_- - 1) + 1/2000\bigr)(1 - \sigma)$: Huxley's exponent at the step's left end
  plus $1/2000$.
\item $g_{\mathrm H}(\sigma) = (101/50)(1 - \sigma)$ for $\sigma \ge 5/6$.
\end{itemize}
The exponent falls from $2.24448$ at $\beta^\dagger$ to about $2.015$ on the last step before $5/6$; from $5/6$ on it is
$101/50$. On each piece $A(1 - \sigma) \le A(1 - \sigma_-) \le 1$, so $g_{\mathrm H} \le 1$ everywhere, and
$g_{\mathrm H}(1/2) = 1$; Proposition~\ref{prop:type2-gen} asks both of a curve. From $\beta^\dagger$ on, $g_{\mathrm
H}(\sigma) \le (7014/3125)(1 - \beta^\dagger) =
0.49625\ldots$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:curve}
% Lean: SieveCurveHx.counts_hx, SieveCurveHx.ghx_le_one, SieveCurveHx.ghx_half, SieveCurveHx.charDensity_hxT
Under Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, $g_{\mathrm H}$ is a density curve.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A finite union of ranges on each of which the bound holds, with its own constant, is a range on which it holds with
the sum of the constants, so it suffices to prove the bound on each piece.
\begin{itemize}
\item For $\sigma < 7789/10000$: $N^*(\sigma, T; q) \le \varphi(q) \le qT$.
\item On $[7789/10000, 131/168)$ and on each of the fifteen steps: Huxley's estimate \cite{huxley75} over the
  characters mod $q$. His (1.1) is a bound $(Q^2 T/q_0)^{12(1 - \sigma)/5 + \varepsilon}$ for the zeros of the
  $L$-functions of the primitive characters to the moduli up to $Q$ divisible by $q_0$, and his (2.23) has the
  exponent $3/(3\sigma - 1)$ there. Take $Q = q_0 = d$ for each $d \mid q$, the case of one modulus. Each character
  mod $q$ is induced by a primitive character mod some $d \mid q$, with the same zeros in $\operatorname{Re} s > 0$,
  so the sum over $d$ bounds the characters mod $q$, at a cost of the number of divisors of $q$, which is
  $\ll q^\varepsilon$. On a piece $[\sigma_-, \sigma_+]$ the exponent $3/(3\sigma - 1)$ is at most its value at
  $\sigma_-$, and the piece's exponent exceeds that value by $1/2000$, or by about $1.5 \cdot 10^{-4}$ on
  $[7789/10000, 131/168)$;
  since $1 - \sigma \ge 1/6$ there, the excess absorbs this $q^\varepsilon$ and Huxley's own $\varepsilon$.
\item For $\sigma \ge 5/6$: the exponent $101/50$. On $[5/6, 11/12]$ Huxley's exponent $3/(3\sigma - 1)$ is at most
  $2$, so his estimate gives $2 + 1/50$, the excess $1/50$ absorbing the same two factors $q^\varepsilon$, since
  $(1 - \sigma)/50 \ge 1/600$ there; above $11/12$ no character is counted, by Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}.
\end{itemize}
These bounds need no factor $(1 + \log qT)^{10}$; the definition allows it because the formalisation's bounds carry
it.
\end{proof}

The formalisation does not deduce the steps from Huxley's published statements: it proves each step by its own
large-values argument (\S S5).

\subsection*{The curve \texorpdfstring{$g^*$}{g*}}

Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} uses $g_{\mathrm H}$ only; a reader of its proof may go on to Section~\ref{sec:type2}. The
curve $g^*$ serves Theorem~\ref{thm:2110}, in the estimate of Section~\ref{sec:leaf-estimate} on the leaves' region
(Definition~\ref{def:leaf-region}), a region of the exponents of the two variables at a leaf of Harman's comparison.
That estimate bounds both polynomials by orthogonality below $4/7$, and from $4/7$ on it needs a density estimate
below $\beta^\dagger$ too, where $g_{\mathrm H}$ is trivial. The curve $g^*$ is the trivial count below $4/7$,
Ingham's exponent $3/(2 - \sigma)$ up to $5/7$, the exponents that Input~\ref{inp:cgl} gives up to $131/168$, and
$g_{\mathrm H}$ from there on, held constant on short steps and rounded up as $g_{\mathrm H}$ is. Let $\sigma^*_k =
5/7 + k/1680$ for $0 \le k \le 14$ and
$\sigma^*_k = \sigma^*_{14} + (k - 14)/280$ for $14 \le k \le 30$, so that $\sigma^*_{30} = 131/168$, and let
$A_0, \dots, A_{29}$ be the numbers of the table below. The curve $g^*$ is the following.
\begin{itemize}
\item $g^*(\sigma) = 1$ for $\sigma < 4/7$.
\item On $[4/7, 99/140)$, divided into nineteen steps $[\sigma_-, \sigma_+)$ of length $1/140$,
  $g^*(\sigma) = \bigl(3/(2 - \sigma_+) + 1/2000\bigr)(1 - \sigma)$: Ingham's exponent at the step's right end plus
  $1/2000$.
\item $g^*(\sigma) = (700003/300000)(1 - \sigma)$ on $[99/140, 5/7)$: Ingham's exponent at $5/7$, which is $7/3$, plus
  $1/100000$.
\item $g^*(\sigma) = A_k(1 - \sigma)$ on $[\sigma^*_k, \sigma^*_{k+1})$, $0 \le k \le 29$.
\item $g^*(\sigma) = g_{\mathrm H}(\sigma)$ for $\sigma \ge 131/168$: fifteen steps of Huxley's exponent at the step's
  left end plus $1/2000$ up to $5/6$, and $(101/50)(1 - \sigma)$ from $5/6$ on.
\end{itemize}
The $A_k$ are multiples of $10^{-5}$ between $4/(1 + \sigma^*_k) + 3.5 \cdot 10^{-5}$ and
$4/(1 + \sigma^*_k) + 8 \cdot 10^{-5}$:
\[
\begin{array}{r@{\quad}llllll}
k = 0\text{--}5:   & 2.33340 & 2.33258 & 2.33178 & 2.33096 & 2.33016 & 2.32934 \\
k = 6\text{--}11:  & 2.32854 & 2.32772 & 2.32692 & 2.32610 & 2.32532 & 2.32452 \\
k = 12\text{--}17: & 2.32370 & 2.32290 & 2.32212 & 2.31728 & 2.31252 & 2.30774 \\
k = 18\text{--}23: & 2.30300 & 2.29826 & 2.29358 & 2.28889 & 2.28423 & 2.27959 \\
k = 24\text{--}29: & 2.27493 & 2.27035 & 2.26575 & 2.26117 & 2.25661 & 2.25207
\end{array}
\]
The exponent $4/(1 + \sigma)$, at the step's left end, is that of the first of the four terms of Chen, Gupta and Li's
density estimate \cite[Theorem~1.2]{cgl} at $q_1 = q$; the library does not prove their theorem, and the $A_k$ come
from its own deduction from Input~\ref{inp:cgl} (below).

The exponent of $g^*$ rises from $840199/398000 = 2.11105\ldots$ on $[4/7, 81/140)$ to $7/3 + 1/100000$ on
$[99/140, 5/7)$, is $11667/5000 = 2.3334$ on $[\sigma^*_0, \sigma^*_1)$, its largest, and then falls, to $2.25207$
before $131/168$ and to about $2.015$ before $5/6$. So $g^*(\sigma) \le (11667/5000)(1 - \sigma)$ for $\sigma \le 1$
(\lean{SieveCurve7D.g7d\_le\_lin}).

Below $\beta^\dagger$ the curve $g^*$ is
at most $g_{\mathrm H}$, which is $1$ there, and smaller from $4/7$ on; on $[\beta^\dagger, 131/168)$ it is larger,
$2.25207(1 - \sigma)$ against $2.24448(1 - \sigma)$; from $131/168$ on the two are equal. Figure~\ref{fig:density}
shows the
exponents of both curves against the classical ones.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\input{fig-density}
\caption{The density exponents $g(\sigma)/(1 - \sigma)$ of the two curves: $g_{\mathrm H}$ (solid), which the
Type~II estimate uses from $\beta^\dagger = 0.7789$ on, and $g^*$ (dashed), which the estimate on the leaves' region
(Section~\ref{sec:leaf-estimate}) uses from $4/7$ on; from $131/168$ on they are equal. Below $\beta^\dagger$ the
exponent of $g_{\mathrm H}$ is $1/(1 - \sigma)$,
which leaves the plot; the dotted arrow marks where $g_{\mathrm H}$ comes back. In gray, the classical exponents:
Ingham's $3/(2 - \sigma)$, Huxley's $3/(3\sigma - 1)$ and Chen, Gupta and Li's $4/(1 + \sigma)$. The classical
deduction gives every Linnik exponent above the largest density exponent on $[1/2, 11/12]$: $12/5$, at $\sigma =
3/4$, with Huxley's estimate, and $7/3$, at $\sigma = 5/7$, with Chen, Gupta and Li's; the largest step of $g^*$,
$11667/5000$, lies just above $7/3$. Beyond $11/12$ there is no zero (Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}).}
\label{fig:density}
\end{figure}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:curve-star}
% Lean: SieveCurve7D.counts_7d, SieveCurve7D.g7d_le_one, SieveCurve7D.g7d_half (the curve SieveCurve7D.g7d)
Under Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}, $g^*$ is a density curve. Moreover $g^* \le 1$ and
$g^*(1/2) = 1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$g^*(1/2) = 1$ by the first item of the definition, and $g^* \le 1$ since on each piece $A(1 - \sigma) \le A(1 -
\sigma_-) < 1$: the largest $A(1 - \sigma_-)$, on $[4/7, 81/140)$, is below $0.9048$. For the first claim, as in the
proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:curve}, it suffices to prove the
bound on each piece. The density steps below carry a factor $(\log qT)^{10}$, which is at most $(1 + \log qT)^{10}$;
the other pieces need none.
\begin{itemize}
\item For $\sigma < 4/7$: $N^*(\sigma, T; q) \le \varphi(q) \le qT$.
\item On each step of $[4/7, 5/7]$: Ingham's estimate \cite{ingham}, in the form over the characters mod $q$. Huxley
  cites Theorem~12.1 of Montgomery's book \cite{montgomery-topics} as a bound of this kind over the primitive
  characters to the moduli up to $Q$ divisible by $q_0$ \cite[p.~435]{huxley75}; with $Q = q_0 = d$ for each $d \mid
  q$ such a bound gives the characters mod $q$, as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:curve}. On a step
  $[\sigma_-, \sigma_+]$ Ingham's estimate bounds the count by
  $C(qT)^{(3/(2 - \sigma_+) + 4\varepsilon)(1 - \sigma)} (\log qT)^{10}$, by zero detection, the mean value theorem
  for the Dirichlet polynomials and the hybrid fourth moment of the $L$-functions for the integrals. The library
  proves it with the exponent $2 + 4c + 4\varepsilon$, $0 \le c \le 1$, under a condition at $\sigma_+$, linear in
  $c$, that holds exactly when $2 + 4c \ge 3/(2 - \sigma_+)$; each step takes $2 + 4c = 3/(2 - \sigma_+)$. On the
  first nineteen steps
  $\varepsilon = 1/8000$, so $4\varepsilon = 1/2000$; on the last $c = 1/12$, $2 + 4c = 7/3$ and
  $\varepsilon = 1/400000$, so $4\varepsilon = 1/100000$.
\item On each step $[\sigma^*_k, \sigma^*_{k+1}]$ of $[5/7, 131/168]$: Input~\ref{inp:cgl}, through a density step
  of Huxley's kind with Input~\ref{inp:cgl} in place of Huxley's large values estimate. We outline it; the
  bookkeeping of the constants, the divisor bound and the Taylor terms is only in the library
  (Appendix~\ref{app:claims}).

  At the scale $Q = q(T + 6) \le 4qT$, a zero detector with a mollifier of length about $Q^{1/2000}$ and a
  smoothing at $Q^{201/400}$ puts each non-trivial character with a zero of real part at least $\sigma$ and imaginary
  part at most $T$ in absolute value into one of two kinds. Let $e = 10^{-5}$, the step's own $\varepsilon$.

  In the integral kind a short integral of $|L(1/2 + it, \chi)|$ is large, and the hybrid fourth moment bounds the
  number of these characters by a constant times $Q^{(201/100)(1/2 - \sigma) + 1/500 + 1 + e}$, which is at most
  $Q^{A_k(1 - \sigma)}$ on the step.

  In the polynomial kind a Dirichlet polynomial with coefficients at most the divisor function is at least $1/4$ at a
  point $\beta_\chi + i\gamma_\chi$ with $\sigma \le \beta_\chi < 1$ and $|\gamma_\chi| \le T$. These characters are
  cut by the dyadic blocks of the polynomial and into bins of the real parts $\beta_\chi$ of width $e/200$. A block of
  length $P$
  is raised to a power $\kappa \le 1556$ with $Q^{\alpha_k} \le P^\kappa \le Q^{\alpha'_k}$, where $\alpha_k \ge 2/3$ and
  $\alpha'_k$ are exponents chosen for the step. Each dyadic piece of the power, of length $N$, is counted by (CGL)
  at the threshold $V = N^\sigma/Z$, carried to the points off the line by a Taylor expansion of $n^{-\delta}$,
  $0 \le \delta \le 1/16$. Here $Z \le N^{1/70}$ is a loss that depends on $\kappa$, on the divisor bound and on the
  number of Taylor terms; since $5/7 \le \sigma \le 4/5$, the threshold lies in $[N^{7/10}, N^{4/5}]$.

  The exponents of the three terms of (CGL) are affine in $\log_Q N$, so it suffices that each is at most
  $A_{k,\mathrm i}(1 - \sigma) - e/4$ at $N = Q^{\alpha_k}$ and at $N = Q^{\alpha'_k}$, where $A_{k,\mathrm i}$ is the
  step's exponent before the cost of the blocks: six conditions, affine in $\sigma$, checked at the step's two ends.
  Then $A_k = A_{k,\mathrm i} + 10^{-5}$ pays for the number of dyadic blocks, at most $Q^{e/(24 \cdot 1556)}$; the bins,
  $\lfloor 60/e \rfloor + 1$ of them, cost a constant, and the trivial character adds one. The parameters of each step
  are chosen
  by a computation to minimise the six exponents at the step's ends, and $A_{k,\mathrm i}$ is the least multiple of
  $10^{-5}$ that they allow.
\item For $\sigma \ge 131/168$, where $g^* = g_{\mathrm H}$: as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:curve}, with
  Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} above $11/12$.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} uses $g_{\mathrm H}$ in the estimate in two variables on the region $\mathcal R_L$ of
Definition~\ref{def:region} (Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}) and in the estimate in one variable of the resonant window
(Section~\ref{sec:window}), and $g^*$ only in the estimate on the leaves' region (Section~\ref{sec:leaf-estimate}).
Input~\ref{inp:cgl} enters Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} twice: through $g^*$, whose steps on $[5/7, 131/168]$ it gives, and
directly, through the large values of the powers of a prime sum (Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers}), which count the classes of
the resonant window.

\section{The Type II estimate in two variables}\label{sec:type2}

The sieve of Section~\ref{sec:type1} needs, for the integers $n = n_1 n_2 r$ with $n_1$ and $n_2$ in ranges near $q$
and $r$ a product of primes, that the non-trivial characters contribute less than the main term. This section proves
it in the form of Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}, a bound for the sum over the non-trivial characters of two Dirichlet
polynomials times a product of prime sums.

Fix a smooth real function $f_0$ supported in $[1/2, 2]$. For a character $\chi$, a scale $Q \ge 4$ and a
height $h \in \mathbb R$, the smoothed prime sum is
\[
  S_Q(\chi, h) = \sum_{p \le 2Q} \chi(p) \Bigl(\frac{p}{Q}\Bigr)^{-ih} f_0\Bigl(\frac{p}{Q}\Bigr).
\]
For two finite sets $S_1 \subseteq (P_1, 2P_1]$ and $S_2 \subseteq (P_2, 2P_2]$ of integers, coefficients $a_m, b_m \in
\mathbb C$ and heights $t_1, t_2 \in \mathbb R$, let
\[
  W_1(\chi) = \sum_{m \in S_1} a_m \chi(m) m^{-it_1}, \qquad W_2(\chi) = \sum_{m \in S_2} b_m \chi(m) m^{-it_2},
\]
and $V_1 = \sum_{m \in S_1} |a_m|^2$, $V_2 = \sum_{m \in S_2} |b_m|^2$. Write $\mathcal X^*$ for the non-trivial
characters mod $q$. Theorem~\ref{thm:type2} below holds on the following explicit region of the exponents
$m_i = \log_q P_i$ of the two lengths.

\begin{definition}\label{def:region}
% Lean: SieveTwoZone7D.Zone7d
Let $L$ be real, $\lambda = 27637/62500 = 0.442192$ and $\mu = 1/12500$. The \emph{region} $\mathcal R_L$ is the set of
the pairs $(m_1, m_2)$ of reals such that, with $s_i = 1 - m_i$ and $\rho = L - m_1 - m_2$,
\[
  \rho \ge \frac1{2000}, \quad m_1, m_2 \ge \frac23, \quad s_1 + \mu \le \lambda\rho, \quad s_2 + \mu \le \lambda\rho,
  \quad s_1 + s_2 + \mu \le \lambda\rho.
\]
\end{definition}

For $P_i = q^{m_i}$, the number $\rho$ is the exponent of the rest $q^L/(P_1 P_2)$, the length of the product of the
prime sums. Two variables of length at least $q$ ($s_i \le 0$) are in the region as soon as $\rho \ge 1/2000$. A
variable of length $q^{1 - s_i}$ shorter than $q$ needs $s_i \le \lambda\rho - \mu$, and the two variables together
need $s_1 + s_2 \le \lambda\rho - \mu$. Since $s_1 + s_2 = 2 - L + \rho$, the last condition is $\rho \le (L - 2 -
\mu)/(1 - \lambda)$, so for each $L$ the region is a bounded polygon. The number $\lambda$ is $2(1 - \beta^\dagger - 4
\cdot 10^{-6})$ at the threshold $\beta^\dagger = 7789/10000$ of Section~\ref{sec:inputs}, where $4 \cdot 10^{-6}$ is
the bound for $\varpi$ in Definition~\ref{def:zone}. Here $\varpi$ is the excess of the prime sums' exponent over the
lower end of a bin, and the bins of Section~\ref{sec:bins} have $\varpi < 2.9 \cdot 10^{-6}$, within this bound. The
number $\mu$ is twice the saving $1/25000$ that each bin must make. Section~\ref{sec:region} explains both.

\begin{theorem}[the Type~II estimate in two variables]\label{thm:type2}
% Lean: SieveFamilyTwo.typeIITwoAt_hx, SieveTwoZone7D.typeII_two_hx
Assume Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}. Let $L$ be real, let $K_0 \ge 0$ be an integer, $e_1 > 0$ and $0 < \eta \le
10^{-5}$. There is $q_0$, depending on $f_0$, $K_0$, $e_1$, $\eta$ and $L$, such that the following holds for every
$q \ge q_0$. Let $P_1, P_2 \ge 1$ be integers with $(\log_q P_1, \log_q P_2) \in \mathcal R_L$, let $S_i \subseteq
(P_i, 2P_i]$, and let the coefficients and the heights $t_1, t_2$ be arbitrary. Let $K \le K_0$, and let $Q_1, \dots,
Q_K \ge q^{e_1}$ and $|h_1|, \dots, |h_K| \le q^\eta$ with $Q_1 \cdots Q_K \le 2^{K_0} q^L/(P_1 P_2)$. Then
\[
  \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X^*} |W_1(\chi)|\, |W_2(\chi)| \prod_{j=1}^K |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)|
  \le q^{L - 1/50000} \Bigl(\frac{V_1}{P_1}\Bigr)^{1/2} \Bigl(\frac{V_2}{P_2}\Bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
\end{theorem}

By Cauchy--Schwarz, $|W_i(\chi_0)| \le P_i (V_i/P_i)^{1/2}$, and $Q_1 \cdots Q_K \le 2^{K_0} q^L/(P_1P_2)$, so the
trivial character's term is at most a constant times $q^L (V_1/P_1)^{1/2}(V_2/P_2)^{1/2}$; the bound saves the power
$q^{-1/50000}$ against it. The proof has three parts, as for one Dirichlet polynomial: the
prime sums are bounded through the zeros of each character's $L$-function (Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum}), the
characters are sorted by those zeros into bins (Section~\ref{sec:bins}), and in each bin the sum over the characters
is bounded. The difference is in the third part. In each bin, Cauchy--Schwarz is taken between $W_1$ and $W_2$, and
each mean square is bounded by the better of orthogonality and the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality in mean square
(Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds}). The number of characters in a bin enters only the second bound, multiplied by a
bound for the kernel $k_Y$; so a density estimate is needed only where the bins are small, and there the classical
one suffices. Section~\ref{sec:zone}
reduces the theorem to a condition on the pairs of lengths, the zone condition, and Section~\ref{sec:region} checks
it on $\mathcal R_L$ through finitely many linear inequalities.

\subsection{Prime sums in a zero-free rectangle}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:prime-sum}
% Lean: SieveWeight.norm_Sp_le
Assume Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}. For every integer $J \ge 0$ there are $B, C \ge 0$ such that the following holds
for every $q \ge 1$, every
$\chi \in \mathcal X^*$, every $1/2 \le \theta \le 11/12$, $0 < \delta \le 1/24$, $H \ge 0$, $Q \ge 4$ and
$y \in \mathbb R$. If $L(s, \chi) \ne 0$ for $\operatorname{Re} s > \theta$, $|\operatorname{Im} s - y| < H + 1$,
then
\begin{multline*}
  |S_Q(\chi, y)| \le M_\delta(\theta)\, \ell_{q, y}\, B\,
  \frac{Q^{\theta + \delta} + Q^{11/12 + \delta}(1 + H)^{-J}}{\log Q} \\
  + C\bigl(\sqrt{2Q} + 1\bigr)\bigl(\log_2 (2Q) + 1\bigr),
\end{multline*}
where $\ell_{q, y} = \log(q + 2) + \log(|y| + 4) + 1$, and $M_\delta(\theta) = C_{\log}(\theta, \theta + \delta) +
C_{\log}(11/12, 11/12 + \delta)$ depends only on $\theta$ and $\delta$; $C_{\log}$ is the constant of the bound for
$L'/L$ in \S S1.
\end{lemma}

The argument is the classical one for primes in progressions \cite[Chapter~5]{iwaniec-kowalski}, with a smooth weight in
place of a sharp cut-off:
Perron's formula for $-L'/L$ against the Mellin transform of the weight, a bound for $L'/L$ in zero-free discs by a
lemma of Landau's type, and a contour that runs along $\operatorname{Re} s = \theta + \delta$ at the heights near $y$
and along $\operatorname{Re} s = 11/12 + \delta$ elsewhere. The only use of Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} is to move the
line of integration to $\operatorname{Re} s = 11/12 + \delta$ and to bound $L'/L$ on that line and to its right.
\S S1 gives the proof.

\subsection{The bins}\label{sec:bins}

Let $I = 500000$ and $\beta_i = 1/2 + 5i/(12I)$ for $0 \le i \le I$, so that $\beta_0 = 1/2$, $\beta_I = 11/12$ and
the bins have width $1/1200000$. For a height bound $T \ge 2$ the \emph{bin} of $\chi \in \mathcal X^*$ is the largest
$i < I$ such that $L(s, \chi)$ has a zero with $\operatorname{Re} s > \beta_i$ and $|\operatorname{Im} s| \le T$, and
$0$ if there is none. If $\chi$ is in the bin $i$ then:
\begin{itemize}
\item $L(s, \chi)$ has no zero with $\operatorname{Re} s > \beta_{i+1}$ and $|\operatorname{Im} s| \le T$: for
  $i + 1 < I$ by the choice of $i$, and for $i + 1 = I$ by Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}. So
  Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum} applies with $\theta = \beta_{i+1}$ to every height $|y| \le T - H - 1$.
\item For $i \ge 1$, $\chi$ is counted by $N^*(\beta_i, T; q)$, so a density curve $g$ (Definition~\ref{def:curve})
bounds the number of characters
  in the bin by $C(qT)^{g(\beta_i)}(1 + \log qT)^{10}$. For $i = 0$ the bound $\varphi(q) \le qT$ is the same, with
  $g(1/2) = 1$ and $\max(C, 1)$ in place of $C$, which the bound for every $i$ then uses.
\end{itemize}
The bins depend on $T$, and so on all the zeros up to the height $T$, but not on the heights $h_j$; the density curve is
therefore used only for the zeros up to the height $T \le 3q^\eta$, a small power of $q$.

The proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:type2-gen} takes from the bins the count of the second item and the following
bound. For the prime sums of Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}, for large $q$ and every $\chi$ in the bin $i$,
\[
  |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le 2 Q_j^{\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta}, \qquad
  \prod_{j=1}^K |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le 4^{K_0} \Bigl(\frac{q^L}{P_1P_2}\Bigr)^{\beta_i + \varpi},
\]
with $\delta = 10^{-6}$ and $\varpi = 1/1200000 + 2\delta < 2.9 \cdot 10^{-6}$. To prove it, take $H = q^\eta$ and
$T = 2H + 1 \le 3q^\eta$, so that every height $|h_j| \le q^\eta$ has $|h_j| + H + 1 \le T$, and apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum} with $J = \lceil 5(1 + L)/(12\eta) \rceil$. As
$Q_j \ge q^{e_1} \ge 4$ for large $q$ and $Q_1 \cdots Q_K \le 2^{K_0} q^L/(P_1P_2) \le 2^{K_0} q^L$, each $Q_j \le
q^{1 + L}$ for large $q$, so that $Q_j^{11/12 + \delta}(1 + H)^{-J} \le Q_j^{1/2 + \delta}$. The hypothesis $Q_j \ge
q^{e_1}$ gives $\ell_{q, h_j}/\log Q_j \ll 1/e_1$, and $M_\delta$ is bounded on the $I + 1$ values $\beta_i$. This
gives the first bound, and the second follows from it, $K \le K_0$, $Q_1 \cdots Q_K \le 2^{K_0} q^L/(P_1P_2)$ and
$\beta_{i+1} = \beta_i + 1/1200000$.

\subsection{Two bounds for a mean square in a bin}\label{sec:two-bounds}

Let $\mathcal X_i$ be the set of the characters in the bin $i$. Since the prime sums are bounded on $\mathcal X_i$, it
remains to bound $\sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X_i} |W_1(\chi)||W_2(\chi)|$, and by Cauchy--Schwarz over $\mathcal X_i$ this
is at most the geometric mean of the two mean squares $\sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X_i} |W_1(\chi)|^2$ and $\sum_{\chi \in
\mathcal X_i} |W_2(\chi)|^2$. Each has two bounds. Write $W(\chi) = \sum_{m \in S} c_m \chi(m) m^{-ih_0}$ for either
polynomial, with $S \subseteq (P, 2P]$ and $V = \sum_{m \in S} |c_m|^2$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Orthogonality.} For every set $\mathcal X$ of characters mod $q$,
  \[
    \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)|^2 \le \sum_\chi |W(\chi)|^2 \le (P + q) V,
  \]
  summed over all characters mod $q$ in the middle. Indeed
  \[
    \sum_\chi |W(\chi)|^2
    = \varphi(q) \sum_{(b, q) = 1} \Bigl|\sum_{m \in S,\ m \equiv b \bmod q} c_m m^{-ih_0}\Bigr|^2;
  \]
  each residue class has at most $P/q + 1$ elements in $(P, 2P]$, and Cauchy--Schwarz in each residue class and
  $\varphi(q) \le q$ give the bound.
\item \emph{The Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality in mean square} \cite{halasz, montgomery69}, Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq}
  below. It bounds the mean square through the \emph{kernel} $k_Y(\psi) = \sum_{n \ge 1} \psi(n) e^{-n/Y}$ of the
  non-trivial characters $\psi$ mod $q$, at $Y = 2P$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{lemma}[the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality in mean square]\label{lem:hm-sq}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.sum_bin_hm_sq, SieveTypeIIHM.hm_duality_wt
Let $P \ge 1$ be an integer, $S \subseteq (P, 2P]$ a set of integers, $c_m \in \mathbb C$, $h_0 \in \mathbb R$, let
$\mathcal X$ be a set of
characters mod $q$, and let $\mathcal H \ge 0$ bound $|k_{2P}(\psi)|$ for every non-trivial character $\psi$ mod $q$.
Then, with $W$ and $V$ as above, $D = \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)|^2$ satisfies $D \le e V (2P + |\mathcal X|
\mathcal H)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\nu_\chi = \overline{W(\chi)}$, so that $D = \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} \nu_\chi W(\chi) = \sum_{m \in S} c_m
m^{-ih_0} \Phi(m)$ with $\Phi(m) = \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} \nu_\chi \chi(m)$. By Cauchy--Schwarz with the weight
$e^{-m/2P}$, which is at least $e^{-1}$ on $(P, 2P]$,
\begin{multline*}
  D^2 \le e V \sum_{m \in S} e^{-m/2P} |\Phi(m)|^2 \le e V \sum_{m \ge 1} e^{-m/2P} |\Phi(m)|^2 \\
  = e V \sum_{\chi, \chi' \in \mathcal X} \nu_\chi \overline{\nu_{\chi'}}\, k_{2P}(\chi \overline{\chi'}).
\end{multline*}
The diagonal terms are at most $\sum_{n \ge 1} e^{-n/2P} \le 2P$ times $|\nu_\chi|^2$, and their sum is at most $2P
\cdot D$. For $\chi \ne \chi'$ the character $\chi\overline{\chi'}$ is non-trivial, so the other terms are at most
$\mathcal H \bigl(\sum_\chi |\nu_\chi|\bigr)^2 \le \mathcal H |\mathcal X| D$, by Cauchy--Schwarz. So $D^2 \le e V(2P +
|\mathcal X|\mathcal H) D$, and the claim follows, trivially if $D = 0$.
\end{proof}

We use the inequality in mean square. Its usual form bounds the first moment,
$(\sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)|)^2 \le e V (2P |\mathcal X| + |\mathcal X|^2 \mathcal H)$, which follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq} by Cauchy--Schwarz over $\mathcal X$. It gives the mean square through $\sum |W|^2 \le
(\sum |W|)^2$ with the loss of a factor $|\mathcal X|$, or on dyadic level sets of $|W|$ with the loss of a
logarithmic factor; Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq} has neither loss. In it the size of $\mathcal X$ multiplies only
$\mathcal H$, and orthogonality does not involve $|\mathcal X|$ at all. So in a bin of two variables the
number of characters enters only through the second bound, and only multiplied by $\mathcal H$.

\emph{The kernel bound.} We use the following bound for the kernel $k_Y$, proved from Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} in
\S S1. Let $\delta' =
\varepsilon' = 1/1000$. There is $C$ such that for every $q$, every non-trivial character $\psi$ mod $q$ and every
$Y \ge 1$,
\[
  |k_Y(\psi)| \le C Y^{1/12 - \delta'} (2q)^{5/12 + \delta' + 2\varepsilon'}.
\]
Its proof writes $k_Y(\psi) = (2\pi i)^{-1} \int_{(2)} L(s, \psi) \Gamma(s) Y^s \, ds$, moves the line to
$\operatorname{Re} s = 1/12 - \delta'$, and bounds $L(s, \psi)$ there by the functional equation and the Lindelöf-type
bound that Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} gives on $\operatorname{Re} s = 11/12 + \delta'$, as the Riemann hypothesis gives
the Lindelöf hypothesis \cite[\S 14.2]{titchmarsh}. At $Y = 2P$ with $P = q^m$ the exponent of $q$ in this bound is
\[
  b(m) = \frac5{12} + \frac{m}{12} + \delta'(1 - m) + 2\varepsilon',
\]
which is $1/2 + 2\varepsilon'$ at $m = 1$. Partial summation and the Pólya--Vinogradov inequality
\cite[Chapter~23]{davenport} give a bound of the same size without Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}: since the weight
$e^{-n/Y}$ decreases from below $1$ to $0$, $|k_Y(\psi)| \le \max_N |\sum_{n \le N} \psi(n)| \ll_\varepsilon
q^{1/2 + \varepsilon}$ for every non-trivial $\psi$ mod $q$ and every $Y > 0$. The end of Section~\ref{sec:type2}
uses this to show that the estimate does not need Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} for the kernel $k_Y$.

\subsection{The zone condition}\label{sec:zone}

Fix a bin, let $\beta$ be its lower end, and write $P_i = q^{m_i}$ and $\rho = L - m_1 - m_2$. Up to constants,
powers of $1 + \log qT$ and the factor $q^\eta$, the bin has at most $q^{g(\beta)}$ characters, the product of the
prime sums is at most $q^{\rho(\beta + \varpi)}$, and $\mathcal H \le q^{b(m)}$ at $P = q^m$. We call a number $M$ an
\emph{exponent} of the mean square of a polynomial of length $q^m$ over the bin if
\[
  M \ge \max(m, 1) \qquad \text{or} \qquad M \ge \max\bigl(m, g(\beta) + b(m)\bigr).
\]
Either alternative makes the mean square at most $q^{M + \eta} V$, up to constants and powers of $1 + \log qT$: the
first by orthogonality, since $P + q \le 2q^{\max(m, 1)}$, and the second by Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq}, since $2P \le
2q^m$ and the bin's size times $\mathcal H$ is at most a constant times $q^{g(\beta) + b(m) + \eta}$. With exponents
$M_1$ and $M_2$ of the two mean squares, and $V_i^{1/2} = q^{m_i/2}(V_i/P_i)^{1/2}$, the bin contributes at most $q^E
(V_1/P_1)^{1/2}(V_2/P_2)^{1/2}$, up to the same factors, with
\[
  E = \rho(\beta + \varpi) + \frac{m_1 + M_1}2 + \frac{m_2 + M_2}2.
\]

\begin{definition}\label{def:zone}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ZoneOK, SieveTypeIIHM.twoBound, SieveTypeIIHM.SqExp
A set $\mathcal R$ of pairs $(m_1, m_2)$ satisfies the \emph{zone condition} for a curve $g$ at $L$ if for every $1/2
\le \beta \le 11/12$, every $0 \le \varpi \le 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$ and every $(m_1, m_2) \in \mathcal R$ there are
exponents $M_1$ and $M_2$ of the two mean squares, in the sense above with $b(m)$ taken at $\delta' = \varepsilon' =
1/1000$, such that the number $E$ above satisfies $E \le L - 1/25000$.
\end{definition}

Each variable chooses its bound independently, but the two share the bin, and so the value $g(\beta)$.

\begin{lemma}[one bin]\label{lem:bin}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.sum_bin_two, SieveTypeIIHM.bin_bound_two
Let $g \le 1$ be a function and $\mathcal R$ a set satisfying the zone condition for $g$ at $L$. Given $C_0, C_d, C_K
\ge 0$, there is $C'$ such that the following holds. Let $q > 1$, $(m_1, m_2) \in \mathcal R$ with $P_i = q^{m_i}$
integers, $1 \le T \le 3q^\eta$ with $\eta \ge 0$, $1/2 \le \beta \le 11/12$ and $0 \le \varpi \le 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$.
Let $\mathcal X$ be a set of at most $C_d (qT)^{g(\beta)} (1 + \log qT)^{10}$ characters on which $\prod_{j=1}^K
|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le C_0 (q^L/(P_1P_2))^{\beta + \varpi}$, for some $K$, $Q_j$ and $h_j$, and let $\mathcal H_i \le
C_K (2P_i)^{1/12 - \delta'} (2q)^{5/12 + \delta' + 2\varepsilon'}$ bound $|k_{2P_i}(\psi)|$ for every non-trivial
character $\psi$ mod $q$. Then for all $S_i \subseteq (P_i, 2P_i]$, coefficients and heights,
\begin{multline*}
  \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W_1(\chi)|\, |W_2(\chi)| \prod_{j=1}^K |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \\
  \le C' (1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{L - 1/25000 + \eta} \Bigl(\frac{V_1}{P_1}\Bigr)^{1/2} \Bigl(\frac{V_2}{P_2}\Bigr)^{1/2}.
\end{multline*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The left side is at most $C_0 (q^L/(P_1P_2))^{\beta + \varpi}$ times $\sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W_1||W_2|$, which is
at most the geometric mean of the two mean squares. Take the exponents $M_1$ and $M_2$ of the zone condition at
$(\beta, m_1, m_2, \varpi)$. If $M_i$ is an exponent by orthogonality, the mean square is at most $(P_i + q)V_i \le 2
q^{M_i + \eta} V_i$. If it is one by Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq}, then $(qT)^{g(\beta)} \le 3 q^{g(\beta) + \eta}$ since $g
\le 1$ and $T \le 3q^\eta$, and $2^{1/12 - \delta'} 2^{5/12 + \delta' + 2\varepsilon'} \le 4$, so that $|\mathcal
X|\mathcal H_i \le 12 C_d C_K (1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{g(\beta) + b(m_i) + \eta}$, and the mean square is at most $e(2 + 12
C_d C_K)(1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{M_i + \eta} V_i$. In both cases its square root is at most $(e(2 + 12C_dC_K))^{1/2}(1 +
\log qT)^5 q^{(m_i + M_i + \eta)/2} (V_i/P_i)^{1/2}$, and the total exponent of $q$ is $E + \eta \le L - 1/25000 +
\eta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:type2-gen}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.typeII_two_gen
Assume Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}. Let $g$ be a density curve with $g \le 1$ and $g(1/2) = 1$, and let $\mathcal R$
satisfy the zone condition for $g$ at $L$. Then the conclusion of Theorem~\ref{thm:type2} holds with $\mathcal R$ in
place of $\mathcal R_L$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By the bins of Section~\ref{sec:bins} and the kernel bound of Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds}, each bin $i$ satisfies the
hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:bin} at $\beta = \beta_i$ and $\varpi = 1/1200000 + 2\delta$, with constants that do not
depend on $q$, so each contributes at most $C' (1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{L - 1/25000 + \eta}(V_1/P_1)^{1/2}(V_2/P_2)^{1/2}$.
There are $I$ bins, and for large $q$, $I C' (1 + \log qT)^{10} \le q^{1/100000}$, so the total is at most
$q^{L - 1/25000 + \eta + 1/100000}(V_1/P_1)^{1/2}(V_2/P_2)^{1/2}$. Since $\eta \le 10^{-5}$,
$L - 1/25000 + \eta + 1/100000 \le L - 1/50000$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The region}\label{sec:region}

With $s_i = 1 - m_i$ and the excess $\Delta_i = M_i - m_i \ge 0$, and since $L = \rho + m_1 + m_2$, the inequality $E
\le L -
1/25000$ reads
\[
  \Delta_1 + \Delta_2 + \mu \le 2\rho(1 - \beta - \varpi), \qquad \mu = \frac1{12500}.
\]
The least exponents give $\Delta_i = \max(0, s_i)$ by orthogonality, and $\Delta_i = \max(0, g(\beta) - T_* + H_1
s_i)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq}, with
\[
  T_* = \frac12 - 2\varepsilon' = \frac{249}{500}, \qquad H_1 = \frac{11}{12} + \delta'.
\]
So the zone condition asks $\Delta(s_1, \beta) + \Delta(s_2, \beta) + \mu \le 2\rho(1 - \beta - \varpi)$, with the
least excess $\Delta(s, \beta) = \max\bigl(0, \min(s, g(\beta) - T_* + H_1 s)\bigr)$, and it involves $L$ only through
$\rho$. Two consequences.
\begin{itemize}
\item For two variables of length at least $q$, $\Delta(s_i, \beta) = 0$ and the condition is $\rho(1 - \beta -
\varpi) \ge 1/25000$,
  which $\rho \ge 1/2000$ gives at every $\beta \le 11/12$, whatever the curve: no density estimate enters.
\item For a variable shorter than $q$, orthogonality costs $s_i$, and the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality does better
  exactly when $g(\beta) < T_* + (1/12 - \delta')s_i$; for a variable of length about $q$ that is $g(\beta) <
  249/500$. Huxley's exponent gives $(1 - \beta) \cdot 3/(3\beta - 1) \le 249/500$ exactly for $\beta \ge 583/749 =
  0.77837\ldots$.
\end{itemize}
So the density estimate matters only for $\beta$ above this point, and the curve $g_{\mathrm H}$ of
Section~\ref{sec:inputs} takes the trivial count below $\beta^\dagger = 7789/10000$ and Huxley's estimate above,
where $g_{\mathrm H} \le (7014/3125)(1 - \beta^\dagger) = 0.49625\ldots < 249/500$. The threshold is the one for which
the certificate's tests of Section~\ref{sec:decomp} are written: Huxley's estimate alone would allow $\beta^\dagger =
7784/10000$ and $\lambda = 0.443192$, a region about $0.2\%$ wider, which the paper does not use (\S S6).

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:region}
% Lean: SieveTwoZone7D.zone_hx, SieveTwoZone7D.zone7d_orth, SieveTypeIIHM.zonePiece_HH, SieveTypeIIHM.zonePiece_of_ends
For every real $L$, the region $\mathcal R_L$ satisfies the zone condition for $g_{\mathrm H}$ at $L$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every case below is hardest at the largest $\varpi$, since $\rho \ge 0$, so take $\varpi = 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$.

\emph{For $\beta \le \beta^\dagger$}, take both exponents by orthogonality, $\Delta_i = \max(0, s_i)$. Each of the four
choices of the maxima, $\sum_i s_i + \mu \le 2\rho(1 - \beta - \varpi)$ with the sum over a subset of the two
variables, is
hardest at $\beta = \beta^\dagger$, where $2(1 - \beta^\dagger - \varpi) = \lambda$. There the four cases are the
region's inequalities $s_1 + \mu \le \lambda\rho$, $s_2 + \mu \le \lambda\rho$, $s_1 + s_2 + \mu \le \lambda\rho$, and
$\mu \le \lambda\rho$, which follows from $\rho \ge 1/2000$. This holds for every curve, and the cases at
$\beta^\dagger$ hold with no slack: that is how $\lambda$ is chosen.

\emph{For $\beta \ge \beta^\dagger$}, take both exponents by Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq}. On each of the $17$ pieces
$[\beta_-, \beta_+]$ of $g_{\mathrm H}$ from $\beta^\dagger$ to $11/12$, $g_{\mathrm H}(\beta) = A(1 - \beta)$ with $A$
constant, and each of the four choices of the maxima, $\Delta_i \in \{0,\ A(1 - \beta) - T_* + H_1 s_i\}$, makes the
case affine in $\beta$. So it suffices to check the four cases at the two ends of each piece. At an end, a case is a
linear inequality in $(s_1, s_2, \rho)$, with nonnegative coefficients on $s_1$ and $s_2$ and the coefficient $-2(1 -
\beta - \varpi) < 0$ on $\rho$. With the relation $s_1 + s_2 = 2 - L + \rho$ dropped, the six inequalities of the
region define a polyhedron in $(s_1, s_2, \rho)$ that contains every $\mathcal R_L$ written in these coordinates, so
one check covers every $L$. Its
recession directions increase $\rho$ and decrease $s_1$ and $s_2$, along which every case gets
easier, so it suffices to check the cases at the polyhedron's vertices. The $17 \times 2 \times 4 = 136$ cases hold at
the vertices, in exact rational arithmetic (\S S5); each case is also a linear combination of the six inequalities with
nonnegative coefficients, for every $L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}]
By Proposition~\ref{prop:curve}, $g_{\mathrm H}$ is a density curve under Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, and
$g_{\mathrm H} \le 1$ and $g_{\mathrm H}(1/2) = 1$ by its definition (Section~\ref{sec:inputs}). Apply
Proposition~\ref{prop:type2-gen}, under
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, with the region $\mathcal R_L$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:region}.
\end{proof}

For one short variable the region's inequality $s_i + \mu \le \lambda\rho$ cannot be relaxed with $g_{\mathrm H}$:
below $\beta^\dagger$, where $g_{\mathrm H} = 1$, orthogonality gives the least exponent of the short variable's mean
square, so the zone condition as $\beta \to \beta^\dagger$ from below, at $\varpi = 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$, is that
inequality.
The bound $m_i \ge 2/3$ is inactive for $L$ up to about $2.42$, since the other inequalities give $s_i \le 1/3$ there.
Beyond, it caps $s_i$ at $1/3$, so that the vertices of the polyhedron in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:region}
have $\rho \le (2/3 + \mu)/\lambda \approx 1.51$, and the case it is needed for there is the one at $\beta = 11/12$,
where it keeps $\Delta_i = 0$ in the
Hal\'asz--Montgomery case: $(101/50)/12 - T_* + H_1/3 < 0$.

At $L = 43/20$ the region is the quadrilateral of Figure~\ref{fig:region}, with the vertices $(m_1, m_2) \approx
(0.881234, 1)$ and $(1, 0.881234)$, where $\rho = 9370/34863 \approx 0.268766$, and $(1.149641, 0.999859)$ and
$(0.999859, 1.149641)$, where
$\rho = 1/2000$. In exponents of $x = q^L$, the two variables lie in $[0.40987, 0.53472]$, within $0.0697$ of each
other, and the rest has exponent at most $0.125008$. The sieve's comparisons of Section~\ref{sec:type1} are built so
that each sum they compare has a first variable, a second, and a rest of primes, in these ranges.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\input{fig-region}
\caption{The region $\mathcal R_L$ of Definition~\ref{def:region} in the plane of the exponents $(m_1, m_2)$ of the
two lengths $q^{m_1}$ and $q^{m_2}$, with equal scales on the two axes; on the edges, $s_i = 1 - m_i$,
$\rho = L - m_1 - m_2$, $\mu = 1/12500$ and $\lambda = 0.442192$. Filled, with a solid outline: $L = 43/20$. Dashed:
$L = 21/10$, which
lies inside it. Each bounding inequality is written beside its edge; the bounds $m_i \ge 2/3$ do not meet the region.
The corners on the edge $\rho \ge 1/2000$ lie just off the gray lines $m_1 = 1$ and $m_2 = 1$; the other two lie on
them.}
\label{fig:region}
\end{figure}

\subsection*{What the estimate uses}

The kernel bound enters only through $b(m)$ in the Hal\'asz--Montgomery case. With the Pólya--Vinogradov bound in
its place, the case reads $\Delta_i = \max(0, g(\beta) - (1/2 - \varepsilon) + s_i)$, and the $136$ cases of the proof
of
Proposition~\ref{prop:region} still hold at the vertices for every $\varepsilon \le 0.0037$, by the same check
(\file{tools/linnik/gen\_zone.py --pv}; not formalised). So the estimate needs
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} for the prime sums, through Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum} and the top bin of
Section~\ref{sec:bins}, and not for the kernel $k_Y$.

No density estimate better than Huxley's would enlarge the region by much. Below $\beta^\dagger$ the density curve is
not used. A smaller exponent at $\beta^\dagger$ and above would let the threshold move down, to the point where
$g(\beta) = 249/500$, and with it raise $\lambda = 2(1 - \beta^\dagger - 4 \cdot 10^{-6})$. Chen, Gupta and Li's
exponent $4/(1 + \sigma)$ \cite{cgl} is larger than Huxley's there. Even the density hypothesis, $A = 2$, would give the
threshold $0.751$ and $\lambda \approx 0.498$. The limit of the method lies elsewhere: in the number of the sieve's
terms that fall outside the region (Section~\ref{sec:comparison}).

In one variable the bound is different in kind. There Cauchy--Schwarz is taken over the bin, so the bin's size enters
orthogonality as $|\mathcal X_i|^{1/2} \approx q^{g(\beta)/2}$, and for a polynomial of length about $q$ the condition
becomes $L \ge 1 + A/2$, with $A$ the largest exponent of a density estimate on all of $[1/2, 11/12]$: $11/5$ with
Huxley's $12/5$, $13/6$ with Chen, Gupta and Li's $7/3$. In two variables the bin's size never meets orthogonality,
and the region holds for every
$L$. What the second variable costs is that both lengths must be near $q$, with a rest of primes of length at least
$q^{1/2000}$.

\section{Type I estimates and Harman's sieve in two variables}\label{sec:type1}

Let $2 < L \le 5/2$; Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} takes $L = 43/20$ and Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} takes $L = 21/10$. Let $x =
q^L$ and let $a$ be prime to $q$. The sets of
the sieve are
\[
  \mathcal A = \{n \in (x, 2x] : n \equiv a \ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\}, \qquad
  \mathcal B = \{n \in (x, 2x] : (n, q) = 1\},
\]
whose integers are those of $(N_1, N_2]$ with $N_1 = \lfloor x \rfloor$ and $N_2 = \lfloor 2x \rfloor$.
For a set $\mathcal C$ of integers, a positive integer $r$ and $z \ge 1$, $\mathcal C_r$ is the set of the multiples of
$r$ in $\mathcal C$, and $S(\mathcal C_r, z)$ the number of $n \in \mathcal C_r$ with no prime factor below $z$. When
every prime factor of $r$ is at least $z$, as in every use below, this is the number of multiples $rm$ of $r$ in
$\mathcal C$ whose cofactor $m$ has no prime factor below $z$; the cofactor $m = 1$ counts. With
$z_{\mathrm{top}} = \lceil x^{1/2 + 2^{-40}} \rceil$, an integer of $(N_1, N_2]$ with no prime factor below
$z_{\mathrm{top}}$ is prime for large $q$, so $S(\mathcal A, z_{\mathrm{top}})$ counts primes in $\mathcal A$. The sieve
compares $\mathcal A$ with $\mathcal B/\varphi(q)$ through the weight
\[
  \vartheta(n) = \mathbf 1[n \equiv a] - \frac{\mathbf 1[(n, q) = 1]}{\varphi(q)}
  = \frac1{\varphi(q)} \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X^*} \overline\chi(a) \chi(n),
\]
which is orthogonality: the trivial character cancels, and the other characters carry the difference.

This section states the tools of Harman's sieve \cite{harman83}, \cite[Chapter~3]{harman} in the form the proof uses,
with every exponent an exponent of $q$; Section~\ref{sec:decomp} converts them to exponents of $x$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:type1} is the classical Type~I count, and Lemma~\ref{lem:typeIB} a bilinear form of Type~I information,
proved by orthogonality, Cauchy--Schwarz and Parseval's identity, with no bound for
Kloosterman sums; Lemma~\ref{lem:leaves} applies it at the
leaves of Buchstab's tree. Lemmas~\ref{lem:family-two} to~\ref{lem:term-two} carry Theorem~\ref{thm:type2} to sums over
integers whose primes are cut out by conditions, and Propositions~\ref{prop:harman-two} and~\ref{prop:harman-fill} are
Harman's comparison with them. The proofs that are standard, or long, are in the supplement (\S S2 and \S S3).

Here and below, a \emph{comparison} of tuples of primes is a condition $c + \sum_i e_i \log y_i > 0$ (or $\ge 0$) on
their entries, with a constant term $c$ and coefficients $e_i \in \{-1, 0, 1\}$; its left side is its \emph{form}, and
an index $i$ with $e_i = \pm 1$ is a \emph{pivot} of it. The constant $C_0$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:leaves} (not that of
Lemma~\ref{lem:bin}) bounds the sums of $1/p$ over the ranges $[y, y^2)$, and
$\zeta_k = e^{2C_0} (2C_0^k/k!) \exp(e^{2C_0} \cdot 2C_0^{k + 1}/(k + 1)!)$, which tends to $0$ as the even parameter
$k$ of Brun's sieve grows (\S S3).

\subsection{Type~I}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:type1}
% Lean: SieveCount.card_A, SieveCount.card_B
Let $0 \le N \le N'$ be integers and let $M > 0$ be prime to $q$. The number of $n \in (N, N']$ with $n \equiv a \pmod
q$ and $M \mid n$ is $(N' - N)/(Mq)$ within $1$, and the number of $n \in (N, N']$ prime to $q$ with $M \mid n$ is
$\varphi(q)(N' - N)/(Mq)$ within $\varphi(q)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $(M, q) = 1$, the Chinese remainder theorem gives $c$ with $c \equiv 0 \pmod M$ and $c \equiv a \pmod q$, and
the two conditions $n \equiv a \pmod q$ and $M \mid n$ together say $n \equiv c \pmod{Mq}$. For $r \ge 1$ the number of
$n \in (N, N']$ with $n \equiv c \pmod r$ is $\lfloor (N' - c)/r \rfloor - \lfloor (N - c)/r \rfloor$, which
differs from $(N' - N)/r$ by less than $1$. With $r = Mq$ this is the first count. The integers prime to $q$ form the
$\varphi(q)$ residue classes $b \bmod q$ with $(b, q) = 1$, and adding the first count over these classes gives the
second.
\end{proof}

So $|\mathcal A_M| - |\mathcal B_M|/\varphi(q)$ is at most $2$ in absolute value for every $M$ prime to $q$. At the
leaves of Buchstab's tree these errors add up to at most twice the number of moduli, which is small against the size
$x/q$ of the main terms as long as the number of moduli is $x/q$ times a negative power of $q$; so these counts serve
moduli up to about $x/q$. Beyond $x/q$
the sieve uses Type~I information in a bilinear form, for moduli that are products $d_1 d_2$ of two parts.

\subsection{Type~I beyond \texorpdfstring{$x/q$}{x/q}}

Write $e_q(t) = e^{2\pi i t/q}$. For a weight $w$ on a finite set $S$ of integers, let
\begin{gather*}
  \hat w(h) = \sum_{k \in S} w(k)\, e_q(hk) \quad (h \bmod q), \\
  E_w = \sum_{k \in S} w(k) \mathbf 1[(k, q) = 1] - \frac{\varphi(q)}{q} \sum_{k \in S} w(k).
\end{gather*}

\begin{lemma}[a bilinear Type~I estimate]\label{lem:typeIB}
% Lean: SieveTypeIB.typeIB_bound_wide, SieveSpline.typeIB_spline_wide
Let $\mathcal D_1$ and $\mathcal D_2$ be finite sets of positive integers prime to $q$, with coefficients $\alpha(d_1)$
and $\beta(d_2)$, and let $w$ be a weight on a finite set $S$ of non-negative integers. Let $H \ge 0$ and $D \ge 1$ be
integers with
$\mathcal D_2 \subseteq [1, D]$ and $2H < q$, and suppose that every residue class mod $q$ contains the inverses of at
most $m$ elements of $\mathcal D_1$, that every integer $|t| d$ with $0 < |t| \le H$ and $d \in \mathcal D_2$ has at
most $F$ divisors, and that $W \ge 0$ and $|\hat w(h)| \le W$ at every $h \ne 0$ that is not the residue of an integer
$t$ with $0 < |t| \le H$. Then
\begin{multline*}
  \Bigl|\sum_{d_1, d_2} \alpha(d_1) \beta(d_2) \sum_{k \in S} w(k)\, \vartheta(d_1 d_2 k)\Bigr| \\
  \le \Bigl(\sum |\alpha|^2\Bigr)^{1/2} \Bigl(mF\Bigl(\Bigl\lfloor \frac{2HD}q \Bigr\rfloor + 1\Bigr) \sum |\beta|^2
  \cdot \frac1q \sum_h |\hat w(h)|^2\Bigr)^{1/2} \\
  + W \sum |\alpha| \sum |\beta| + \frac{|E_w|}{\varphi(q)} \Bigl|\sum \alpha \sum \beta\Bigr|.
\end{multline*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $d$ prime to $q$, with $\bar d$ its inverse mod $q$, $\mathbf 1[dk \equiv a] = q^{-1} \sum_h e_q(h(k - a\bar d))$,
and
$(dk, q) = 1$ exactly when $(k, q) = 1$. The frequency $h = 0$ contributes $q^{-1} \sum_k w(k)$, and with the second
part of $\vartheta$ it leaves $-E_w/\varphi(q)$, so that
\begin{gather*}
  \sum_{d_1, d_2} \alpha(d_1)\beta(d_2) \sum_k w(k)\, \vartheta(d_1 d_2 k)
  = \frac1q \sum_{h \ne 0} \hat w(h) B(h) - \frac{E_w}{\varphi(q)} \sum \alpha \sum \beta, \\
  B(h) = \sum_{d_1, d_2} \alpha(d_1) \beta(d_2)\, e_q(-ha \bar d_1 \bar d_2).
\end{gather*}
Since $2H < q$, the residues of the integers $t$ with $0 < |t| \le H$ are distinct. At the other frequencies
$|\hat w(h)| \le W$ and $|B(h)| \le \sum |\alpha| \sum |\beta|$, and there are fewer than $q$ of them, which gives the
second term. At the frequencies $t$, Cauchy--Schwarz over $d_1$ gives
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_t \hat w(t) B(t)\Bigr|^2 \le \sum |\alpha|^2 \cdot
  \sum_{d_1} \Bigl|\sum_{t, d_2} \hat w(t) \beta(d_2)\, e_q(-a t \bar d_2 \cdot \bar d_1)\Bigr|^2,
\]
and as $d_1$ runs over $\mathcal D_1$, $\bar d_1$ runs over the residues mod $q$, each at most $m$ times. Parseval's
identity, $\sum_{s \bmod q} |\sum_j c_j e_q(s f_j)|^2 = q \sum_{f_j \equiv f_{j'}} c_j \bar c_{j'}$, with $j = (t,
d_2)$,
$c_j = \hat w(t)\beta(d_2)$ and $f_j = -at\bar d_2$, bounds the inner sum by $mq$ times the sum of
$|\hat w(t)\beta(d_2)\hat w(t')\beta(d_2')|$ over the pairs with $t \bar d_2 \equiv t' \bar d_2'$, that is
$t d_2' \equiv t' d_2 \pmod q$. Bound each product by $\tfrac12(|\hat w(t)|^2 |\beta(d_2')|^2 + |\hat w(t')|^2
