% Lean: SieveAd.bd_flip_neg, SieveAd.Plain.bd_flip_core, SieveAd.bd_flipCost, SieveAd.Plain.bd_flipCostD, SieveAd.froz_ends_le, SieveAd.froz_top_le, SieveAd.dJF_level, SieveAd.psi_level; the sign +: SieveAd.bd_flip_pos
Let $\mathfrak b$ be a box that may be reversed, with a reversal of cost $c_R$ whose tree passes. Then the term of
$\mathfrak b$ with the sign $-$ is bounded at $c_R/\mathcal K$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $C = c_F/\mathcal K$, $C_e = c_e/\mathcal K$, $w_0 = (h_1/D)\, c_D(\mathfrak b)/\mathcal K$, $w_1 = ((h_1 + 1)/D)\,
c_D(\mathfrak b^{\mathrm{lo}})/\mathcal K$ and $w_2 = ((l_1 + 1)/D)\, c_D(\mathfrak b^{\mathrm{hi}})/\mathcal K$, so
that
$c_R/\mathcal K \ge C(w_0 + w_1 + w_2) + C_e(w_1 + w_2)$. Write $\mathcal M_{\mathrm e}$ for the union of the two ends.
By
Lemma~\ref{lem:sandwich}, applied to $\mathcal A$ for the upper inequality and to $\mathcal B$ for the lower,
\[
  \Delta(\mathfrak b) \le T_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}}(\mathcal A, \emptyset; \nu) - \frac{T_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm
  n}}(\mathcal B, \emptyset; \nu)}{\varphi(q)} = \Delta_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}}(\emptyset; \nu) + \frac{T_{\mathcal
  M_{\mathrm e}}(\mathcal B, \emptyset; \nu)}{\varphi(q)}.
\]

By (\ref{eq:levels}), $\Delta_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}}(\emptyset; \nu) = \Delta_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}}(\emptyset; f) -
\Delta_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}}(((f, \nu)))$. The first term is $o(X)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:frozen-tests}, and by
Lemma~\ref{lem:frozen-tree} with the sign $+$, $-\Delta_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}}(((f, \nu))) \le ((C +
\eta)\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}) + \eta)X$.

For $R$ in the wide part, $y = x/R \in [x^{(l_1 - 1)/D}, x^{h_1/D})$, and the integers of $\mathcal B$ counted for $R$ at
the level $\nu$ are at most $\Phi(y, z_\nu)$; the arguments $\log 2y/\log z_\nu$ lie in $[(l_1 - 1)/(\nu + 1), (h_1 +
2)/\nu)$, and $1/\log z_\nu \le D/(\nu \log x)$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:rough} gives $T_{\mathcal M_{\mathrm e}}(\mathcal B,
\emptyset; \nu)/\varphi(q) \le (C_e + \eta)\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M_{\mathrm e}) X$ for large $q$; the
formalisation uses $h_1 + \nu + 1 \le D$ there. Finally
$\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M_{\mathrm w}) = \operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M_{\mathrm n}) + \operatorname{wt}(\mathcal
M_{\mathrm e})$, and the ends $\mathcal M(D - h_1, D - h_1 + 1)$ and $\mathcal M(D - l_1, D - l_1 + 1)$ are the narrow
parts of $\mathfrak b^{\mathrm{lo}}$ and of $\mathfrak b^{\mathrm{hi}}$, whose largest primes have the cells $(h_1 - 1,
h_1 + 1)$ and $(l_1 - 1, l_1 + 1)$. Lemma~\ref{lem:frozen-weight} applies to the three
boxes, since $h(\mathfrak b^{\mathrm{lo}}) = h(\mathfrak b) + 1 \le D$, $h(\mathfrak b^{\mathrm{hi}}) \le h(\mathfrak
b)$,
$h_1 + 1 \le 2(h_1 - 1)$ and $l_1 + 1 \le 2(l_1 - 1)$ as $h_1 > l_1 \ge 3$, and $h_2 \le l_1 - 1$; it gives
$\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M_{\mathrm n}) \le w_0 + \eta$ and $\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M_{\mathrm e}) \le w_1 + w_2
+ 2\eta$. Collecting, $\Delta(\mathfrak b) \le (C(w_0 + w_1 + w_2) + C_e(w_1 + w_2) + O(\eta))X$, with an $O$ that
depends only on $C$, $C_e$ and the $w_i$.
\end{proof}

The same argument with the narrow part in place of the wide one, and a tree over it on $((f, \nu))$ with the sign $-$,
bounds the term with the sign $+$ at $Cw_0 + C_e(w_1 + w_2)$; Section~\ref{sec:reversal-plus} extends the lemmas above
to the narrow part and proves this (Proposition~\ref{prop:reversal-plus}(a)). The search of
Section~\ref{sec:certificate} accepts a reversal of either sign, and its soundness (Lemma~\ref{lem:search}) covers
both; the certificate of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} reverses boxes of the sign $-$ only, and that of
Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} uses the reversal with the sign $+$, with the cost of Section~\ref{sec:reversal-plus}.

\emph{When the reversal wins.} The reversal costs about $C h_1/D$ times the bound $c_D(\mathfrak b)/\mathcal K$ for
the box's share,
the ends adding little, as they are one unit wide. Here $C$ is the cost of the tree over the frozen part in units of
$\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M)$, against which the main term of the sieve over the frozen part, the primes $P \in (x/R,
2x/R]$, is about $(D/h_1)\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal M)$: so $Ch_1/D$ is the fraction of that main term which the tree
over the frozen part loses. Where Buchstab's identity on a box of the sign $-$ creates terms of the sign $+$ that can
only
be dropped at their full shares, the reversal pays only that fraction of the box's share. It applies only to boxes whose
largest prime is large: the frozen numbers $R$, of size $x/p_1$, are among the variables of the tests over the frozen
part, and $(\mathrm L)$ there asks $a_1 + a_2 < 1 - 1/(2L)$ of its two exponents (Definition~\ref{def:tests}), while
$a_1 + a_2 \ge (D - l_1 + 1)/D$; so the tests at a
floor
pass only for $l_1 > D/(2L) + 1$. The search of Section~\ref{sec:certificate} takes the reversal when its cost is below
that of the decomposition by Buchstab's identity.

\subsection{What a tree gives}

A tree that passes bounds the term of its box (Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-bound}). A floor $f_*$ at which the box of
no prime passes (J3), and a tree that passes on the box $\mathfrak b_0 = ((f_*, D/2 + 1))$ of the first prime, with
the sign $-$ and a cost below $\mathcal K$, give Linnik's theorem with the exponent $L$ and a count of the primes at
$x = q^L$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:tree}). Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} is assumed, as throughout this section.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:tree-bound}
% Lean: SieveAd.sound, SieveAd.bd_split, SieveAd.bd_buch, SieveAd.bd_mono, SieveAd.Plain.rulesMP, SieveAd.Plain.solveD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.altFD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.bd_flipCostD, SieveAd.pr_split, SieveAd.pr_buch
Let $\mathfrak b$ be a box of $k \ge 1$ primes. If a tree on $\mathfrak b$ with the sign $\varepsilon$ passes, with cost
$c$, then the term of $\mathfrak b$ with the sign $\varepsilon$ is bounded at $c/\mathcal K$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By induction on the tree; each case is a finite conjunction of statements for large $q$, which hold together for large
$q$. (V): by Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(c), $\Delta(\mathfrak b) = 0$ for large $q$. (E): by
Lemma~\ref{lem:tests}(a),
$\varepsilon\Delta(\mathfrak b) \ge -|\Delta(\mathfrak b)| \ge -\eta X$. (D) and (P): $\varepsilon = +$ and
$\Delta(\mathfrak b) \ge -T(\mathcal B, \mathfrak b)/\varphi(q)$, which Lemma~\ref{lem:drop} bounds. (S): by
Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(a), $\Delta(\mathfrak b)$ is the sum of the two boxes' $\Delta$, each bounded with the
sign $\varepsilon$ at its cost, with $\eta/2$ for each. (B): by Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(b),
\[
  \varepsilon\Delta(\mathfrak b) = \varepsilon\Delta(\mathfrak b; f) + (-\varepsilon)\Delta(\mathfrak b \oplus (f, h_k))
  \ge -\frac\eta2 X - \Bigl(\frac c{\mathcal K} + \frac\eta2\Bigr) X,
\]
the first term by Lemma~\ref{lem:tests}(b) and the second by the induction, the tree below having the sign
$-\varepsilon$
and the cost $c$. (R): Proposition~\ref{prop:reversal}.
\end{proof}

The tree is finite, so finitely many applications of Lemmas~\ref{lem:tests} and~\ref{lem:drop} and of
Propositions~\ref{prop:harman-two} and~\ref{prop:harman-fill} enter, each with its own $q_0$. The only main term that
the argument computes is that of $S(\mathcal B, z_{\mathrm{top}})$: every other term is compared with its version in
$\mathcal B/\varphi(q)$, dropped at the cost of that version, or bounded through a reversal by such comparisons.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:tree}
% Lean: SieveAd.linnik_pr, SieveAd.linnik_bd, SieveAd.linnik_large_bd, SieveAd.linnik_tree, SieveAd.Ttop_eq, SieveAd.Ttop_gB_ge, SieveMain2.route_of_large, SieveAd.count_large_bd, SieveAd.count_large_pr
Let $2 < L \le 5/2$, and let $f_* \le D/2 + 1$ be a floor at which the box of no prime passes (J3). Suppose that the
term of the box $\mathfrak b_0 = ((f_*, D/2 + 1))$ of one prime with the sign $-$ is bounded at some $C < 1$, as it is
with $C = c/\mathcal K$ if $\mathfrak b_0$ has a tree with the sign $-$ that passes, with cost $c < \mathcal K$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] there is $K$ such that for every $q \ge 1$ and every $a$ prime to $q$ there is a prime $p \equiv a \pmod q$
  with $p \le Kq^L$;
\item[(b)] for large $q$ and every $a$ prime to $q$, the primes $p \equiv a \pmod q$ with $q^L < p \le 2q^L$ number at
  least $(1 - C)X/2$, where $X = q^L/(\varphi(q) \log q^L)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\eta = (1 - C)/8$, and let $q$ be large and $a$ prime to $q$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(d) for $\mathcal A$ and for $\mathcal B$,
\[
  S(\mathcal A, z_{\mathrm{top}}) - \frac{S(\mathcal B, z_{\mathrm{top}})}{\varphi(q)} = \Delta(\emptyset; f_*) -
  \Delta(\mathfrak b_0), \qquad \mathfrak b_0 = ((f_*, D/2 + 1)).
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:tests}(b), $|\Delta(\emptyset; f_*)| \le \eta X$, and by the hypothesis (with
Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-bound} for a tree), $-\Delta(\mathfrak b_0) \ge -(C + \eta)X$. A prime of $(x, 2x]$ exceeds
$q$, so it is prime to $q$, and it has
no prime factor below $z_{\mathrm{top}} \le N_1$; so $S(\mathcal B, z_{\mathrm{top}})$ is at least the number of primes
of $(x, 2x]$, which is at least $(1 - \eta)x/\log x$ by the prime number theorem. So
\[
  S(\mathcal A, z_{\mathrm{top}}) \ge (1 - \eta)X - \eta X - (C + \eta)X > \frac{1 - C}2 X > 0.
\]
An integer counted by $S(\mathcal A, z_{\mathrm{top}})$ is at most $N_2 < z_{\mathrm{top}}^2$ and has no prime factor
below $z_{\mathrm{top}}$, so it is a prime $p \equiv a \pmod q$ with $q^L < p \le 2q^L$. So these primes number at
least $S(\mathcal A, z_{\mathrm{top}}) > (1 - C)X/2 > 0$. This gives $q_0$ such that the theorem holds for $q \ge q_0$
with $K = 2$. Each of the finitely many pairs $(q, a \bmod q)$ with $q < q_0$ has a prime
$p \equiv a \pmod q$ by Dirichlet's theorem, and $K$ is taken large enough to cover them.
\end{proof}

The theorem holds for every such $L$ with the tests as defined. For rational $L$ the tests are inequalities between
integers, which is how the formalisation states them and how Lean's kernel evaluates them. The formalisation proves
the theorem for rational $L$, and at $L = 43/20$ with $f_*$ and the bound of Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate}.

\subsection{From \texorpdfstring{$x = q^L$}{x = q\textasciicircum L} to every \texorpdfstring{$x$}{x}}\label{sec:every-x}

The count (b) of Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} is at $x = q^L$. The following lemma does not use the conventions of this
section: in it $L > 0$ is arbitrary, $x$ is any real number with $x \ge Kq^L$, not $q^L$, and no input is assumed.
It is a statement about primes in progressions alone, and uses no sieve.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:every-x}
% Lean: SieveAd.count_of_large, SieveAd.card_units_above (with Theorem thm:tree: SieveAd.count_bd, SieveAd.count_pr)
Let $L > 0$, $c_0 \ge 0$ and $q_0 \ge 2$. Suppose that for every modulus $Q \ge q_0$ and every $b$ prime to $Q$ the
primes $p \equiv b \pmod Q$ with $Q^L < p \le 2Q^L$ number at least $c_0 Q^L/(\varphi(Q) \log Q^L)$. Let $K =
\max(2^{L + 2}, 2(2q_0)^L)$. Then for every $q \ge 1$, every $a$ prime to $q$ and every $x \ge Kq^L$,
\[
  \pi(x; q, a) \ge \frac{c_0}{2^{L + 1}} \cdot \frac{x}{\varphi(q) \log x}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $q \ge 1$, $x \ge 2^{L + 2} q^L$ and $x \ge 2(2q_0)^L$. Let $t = (x/2)^{1/L}$, so that $t^L = x/2$. As $x/2
\ge 2^{L + 1} q^L \ge (2q)^L$, $x/2 \ge (2q_0)^L$ and $y \mapsto y^L$ increases on $[0, \infty)$, $t \ge 2q$ and $t
\ge 2q_0$. Let $m = \lfloor t/q \rfloor$ and $Q = qm$. Then $Q \le t$, and $Q > t - q \ge t/2$ since $q \le t/2$. So
$Q \ge q_0$, $Q \ge q \ge 1$, $q \mid Q$, and
\[
  \frac x{2^{L + 1}} = \Bigl(\frac t2\Bigr)^L \le Q^L \le t^L = \frac x2;
\]
moreover $x/2^{L + 1} \ge 2q^L \ge 2$, so $Q^L \ge 2$.

Reduction mod $q$ is a homomorphism from the group of units mod $Q$ to that of units mod $q$, since $q \mid Q$. It is
onto. Let $s$ be the product of the primes that divide $Q$ but neither $q$ nor $a$, and $b = a + qs$. A prime factor
of $Q$ that divides $q$ divides $qs$ and not $a$; one that divides $a$ but not $q$ divides neither $q$ nor $s$, so
not $qs$; any other divides $qs$ and not $a$. None of them divides $b$, so $b$ is prime to $Q$, and $b \equiv a
\pmod q$. So the unit classes mod $Q$ above $a$ form a coset of the kernel of this reduction, and there are
$\varphi(Q)/\varphi(q)$ of them. A prime $p \equiv b \pmod Q$ with $b$ above $a$ has $p \equiv a \pmod q$, since $q
\mid Q$. A prime lies in one class mod $Q$ only, so the primes of different classes are different.

By the hypothesis at $Q \ge q_0$, each class above $a$ has at least $c_0 Q^L/(\varphi(Q) \log Q^L)$ primes in $(Q^L,
2Q^L]$, and they are at most $2Q^L \le x$. Summing over the classes,
\[
  \pi(x; q, a) \ge \frac{\varphi(Q)}{\varphi(q)} \cdot \frac{c_0 Q^L}{\varphi(Q) \log Q^L}
  = \frac{c_0}{\varphi(q)} \cdot \frac{Q^L}{\log Q^L}.
\]
Finally $2 \le Q^L \le x/2 < x$ gives $0 < \log Q^L \le \log x$, and with $Q^L \ge x/2^{L + 1}$ this gives $Q^L/\log
Q^L \ge (x/2^{L + 1})/\log x$.
\end{proof}

The lemma uses its hypothesis for every modulus $Q \ge q_0$ and every class prime to $Q$: $Q$ is the multiple of $q$
that $q$ and $x$ determine, and every class above $a$ is counted. So a lower bound that is proved for some moduli
only does not extend to every $x \ge Kq^L$ in this way. The formalisation states that such a $K$ exists; its proof
takes the value above.

\section{The certificate}\label{sec:certificate}

Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} reduces Linnik's theorem with the exponent $L$ to a finite computation: a floor $f_*$ at which
the box of no prime passes (J3), and a bound below $1$ for the term of the root box $\mathfrak b_0 = ((f_*, D/2 + 1))$
with the sign $-$, which a tree of cost below $\mathcal K$ gives. At $L = 43/20$ the tree has some $3.4 \cdot 10^6$
nodes, those of the trees over the frozen parts of its reversals included. It is found by a search run outside Lean's
kernel; that search tries what a strategy proposes and needs no proof, and the choices it keeps, the plan, are written
down as hints. Lean's kernel runs a search led by the hints, which chooses nothing, on the \emph{parts of the
certificate}: ranges of cells of the first, second or third prime. It checks that the search's values there, joined by
steps of Buchstab's identity, add up to a cost below $\mathcal K$. This section describes the search, why its value is
a proof whatever the strategy or the hints (Lemma~\ref{lem:search}), the hints, and the numbers at $43/20$;
Section~\ref{sec:certificate-b} has the search and the numbers at $21/10$.

\subsection{The search}\label{sec:search}

The search takes a box, a sign and a \emph{limit}, an integer, and returns an integer: a value below the limit is a
cost at which the term is bounded, and a value at least the limit says nothing. What it tries at a box, the floors,
the configurations and the cuts, is proposed by a \emph{strategy}, described below, which needs no proof: every
proposal is tested before it is used. A \emph{configuration} is the choice of the sets and the side that a test of
Definition~\ref{def:tests} takes. At a box the search acts as follows, in this order; where two nodes are possible, it
keeps the cheaper.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If the box is empty, (V), or a proposed configuration passes the test (I), it returns $0$.
\item If the box may be dropped, (D) or (P), it returns the smaller of the drop's cost and the cost of its decomposition
  by Buchstab's identity, the latter searched with the smaller of the drop's cost and the limit as its limit.
\item Otherwise, if the box has fewer primes than the strategy's \emph{depth}, it decomposes the box at the first
  proposed floor at which (J2) or (J3) passes: the new prime's range $[f, h_k)$ is cut at the proposed cuts, and each
  cell is searched with the limit that the cells before it leave; the search of the range stops as soon as the costs
  reach the limit.
\item A box that may be reversed is reversed instead if that costs less than the decomposition. At $43/20$ the
  strategy proposes this for the boxes of the sign $-$ only; the search accepts a reversal of either sign, by the
  remark after Proposition~\ref{prop:reversal}.
\item A box that can be neither decomposed nor reversed is cut in two at the middle of its newest cell, if that cell
  is wider than a least width that the strategy fixes.
\item Otherwise the search returns a value at least the limit, which says nothing.
\end{enumerate}
A reversal is searched in the same way. Its floor is the first proposed floor at which the chain of no prime passes a
test over the frozen part. The tree over the frozen part on $((f, \nu))$ is searched with the nodes of
Definition~\ref{def:frozen-tree}, and with the limit past which the reversal's cost (\ref{eq:cR}) would not be below
that of the decomposition. The search that the kernel follows searches it with the fixed limit $64\mathcal K$
instead, which no tree of the plan reaches.

At $43/20$ the strategy proposes the following.
% The strategy is the planner's (tools/linnik/hint_cert.py, on flip_cert.py and fill_cert.py; the plan carries its choices). In Lean, what its proposals are made of: SieveAd.fmax3, SieveAd.bestSub, SieveAd.stepOf; the searches that try: SieveAd.solveG, SieveAd.nodeG, SieveAd.kids.
\begin{itemize}
\item For (I): as the rest, the first prime of the box, from the smallest, whose cell the region admits for a rest and
  for which a set of the other primes has sums that the region admits for the first variable with it; and that set.
\item For (J2): for each prime that the region admits as the rest, the floor $\min(l_{\min}, v - u - 1)$ allowed by its
  admissible range $[u, v]$, with $l_{\min}$ the least lower end of the box's cells; as $\mathcal S_0$ the set of the
  other primes
  of largest lower sum whose upper sum stays below $v$; and a set $\mathcal S_1$ and a side $\iota$ for which
  $(\mathrm L)$ holds, if there are any; of these, the one of highest floor.
\item For (J3): eight floors, $f_3 = \min(l_{\min}, f_{\max})$ and then each three quarters of the one before, rounded
  down ($\lfloor 3f_3/4 \rfloor$, \dots), where $f_{\max}$ is the largest floor with $u + f < v$ in (J3); at each, as
  $\mathcal S_0$ the set of the box's primes of largest lower sum whose upper sum stays below $v$, and the first split
  $(\mathcal S_1, \iota)$ of the leaves that passes $(\mathrm L)$. The first floor with such a split is proposed before
  (J2)'s if it is higher.
\item Cuts of the new prime's range $[f, h_k)$: cells of side at most $4398046511/D$, about $0.004$, for the first four
  primes, twice that for the fifth and sixth, and at most a tenth of their lower end, up to the exponent from which the
  new prime's square makes every box of products only; then the exponent $D - 1 - h(\mathfrak b)$, below which the new
  box has no product in $(x, 2x]$ and may be dropped; and $l_1 - 1$, below which a box may be reversed.
\item Depth $6$: boxes of fewer than six primes are decomposed, so a box has at most six. Reversed: boxes of at most six
  primes, so of three or five, since they have the sign $-$.
\item Over a frozen part: chains of fewer than three primes are decomposed; the chain's primes have cells of side at
  most $6597069767/D$, about $0.006$, twice that for the third, and at most a tenth of their lower end; and the
  configurations are those of the box with the cofactor $m$ as one more cell, whose range is read off the range
  $(e_-, e_+]$ of the frozen part, widened by the room $\gamma$ for the tests at a floor.
\end{itemize}
The cuts are free, since node (S) of Definition~\ref{def:tree} allows a cut at any point: the side $0.004$ and the
depth $6$ affect only the cost.

\subsection{Checking a search}\label{sec:checking}

Lean's kernel can decide a proposition about natural numbers, lists and Booleans by evaluating it, trusting no code
compiled from the definitions; it computes on numerals with the GMP library (\S S5). The search is such a function: its
tests are the conditions of Definitions~\ref{def:tests},
\ref{def:frozen-tree}, \ref{def:reversal} and~\ref{def:reversal-plus} in integers, and its costs are the integers
(\ref{eq:cD}), $c'_D$, (\ref{eq:cR}) and, for a reversal of the sign $+$, (\ref{eq:cRplus}), every division rounded
up, with $\Omega$ computed from its table. Its soundness is proved once, for every strategy.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:search}
% Lean: SieveAd.solveG_sound, SieveAd.kidsP_sound, SieveAd.Plain.solveD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.stepD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.kidsC_sound, SieveAd.Plain.partD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.solveMD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.altFD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.pr_of_partD
Whatever the strategy, or the hints that lead the search (below):
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] if the search returns on a box, a sign and a limit a value $c$ below the limit, then the term of the box
  with that sign is bounded at $c/\mathcal K$;
\item[(b)] let $\mathfrak b$ be a box of $k \ge 0$ primes, $\varepsilon$ a sign and $\alpha_0 < \alpha_1 < \dots <
  \alpha_N$ integers. If the values of the search on the boxes $\mathfrak b \oplus (\alpha_{i - 1}, \alpha_i)$,
  $1 \le i \le N$, with the sign $\varepsilon$, the hints read in turn and each with the limit $\mathcal K$ less
  the values before it, are each below its limit and add up to at most $c < \mathcal K$, then the term of $\mathfrak b
  \oplus (\alpha_0, \alpha_N)$ with the sign $\varepsilon$ is bounded at $c/\mathcal K$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) By induction on the depth of recursion, with the cases of the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-bound}. A value
below the limit comes from a node whose condition the search has evaluated to true:
\begin{itemize}
\item an empty or evaluated box, (V) or (E), at the cost $0$;
\item a dropped box, (D) or (P), at its cost;
\item a decomposition at a floor whose test has passed, (B): the cells' values, each below its limit, are bounds for
  their terms by the induction, and they add up by cuts (S), since any point strictly inside a range is a cut;
\item a reversal whose test at its floor has passed, (R): by the same induction the tree over the frozen part has the
  cost from which (\ref{eq:cR}) is computed; for a box of the sign $+$, which the search led by hints also accepts,
  the cost is that of Section~\ref{sec:reversal-plus}, by the bound stated after Proposition~\ref{prop:reversal};
\item a box cut in two, (S).
\end{itemize}
A branch whose condition fails returns its limit, which says nothing. (b) follows from (a) by induction on $N$: each
value is below its limit, so (a) bounds each
cell's term, and Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(a) adds the bounds.
\end{proof}

When no box of the sign $+$ is reversed, as at $43/20$, the same induction shows that the nodes that the search took
form a tree on the box with that sign that passes, of cost at most $c$; the formalisation proves the bound alone
(\S S5).

So the certificate is the hints together with propositions about integers: that the search's value on each part of
the certificate, with the limit $\mathcal K$, is at most a bound stated with the part; that the steps of Buchstab's
identity that join the parts pass their tests; and that the stated bounds add up to at most $c$. The kernel proves
them by evaluation, part by part, and the formalisation proves the bound of the term that Lemma~\ref{lem:search} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:tests}(b) draw from them.

\subsection{Hints}\label{sec:hints}

A search that tries what its strategy proposes spends most of its time on what it does not keep: trees under boxes
that are then dropped or reversed, the test of every proposed floor where one is used, and the search for a
configuration among the sets of a box's cells. So the search is run once outside the kernel, by a program that
follows its definitions one by one in exact integer arithmetic, and the plan it keeps is written as \emph{hints}: for
each part of the certificate, a natural number whose binary digits, read from the least significant, follow the boxes
in the order in which the search visits them. At a box the hints give a run of ones closed by a zero, which names the
node, and then what the node needs.
% Lean: SieveAd.solveD, SieveAd.stepD, SieveAd.cfgIP, SieveAd.cfgJP, SieveAd.cfgIM, SieveAd.cfgJM, SieveAd.cfgIF, SieveAd.cfgJF, SieveAd.floorP, SieveAd.kidsCS, SieveAd.kidsC, SieveAd.kidsM, SieveAd.kidsF, SieveAd.partD, SieveAd.Plain.altFD, SieveAd.Plain.flipCostD, SieveAd.Plain.flipTreeD, SieveAd.solve21500
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lp{0.68\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Hints & What the search does at the box \\
\midrule
\texttt{0} & evaluates the box by (I), with the configuration that follows \\
\texttt{10} & Buchstab's identity, at the floor and with the configuration that follow, then the cuts of the new
  prime's range \\
\texttt{110} & drops the box \\
\texttt{1110} & reverses the box: the floor and the configuration of the test over the frozen part, then the cuts of
  the range of the chain's first prime \\
\texttt{11110} & cuts the newest cell in two at its middle \\
\texttt{111110} & finds the box empty, (V) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
A configuration of (I) is the rest's cell, $0$ for the smallest prime's or $1$ and three bits for its index, and the
set $\mathcal S$, a bit for each cell of the box. At a floor, four bits name the comparison and with it the floor: with
$2j$, (J2) with the rest the prime at position $j$, counted from the smallest from $0$, at the floor $\min(l_{\min}, v
- u - 1)$ of its admissible range $[u, v]$; with $2i + 1$, (J3) at the floor after $i$ of its eight steps. Then come
$\mathcal S_0$, a bit for each cell, and either $0$, for the side $\iota = 1$ and the set $\mathcal S_1$ of all the
cells but that of the largest prime $p_1$ (over a frozen part, all but the cofactor's), or $1$, the set $\mathcal S_1$
and a bit for the side. Over a frozen part every set has one more bit, the cofactor's, and the rest
of (J2) may be the cofactor.

Each cut of a new prime's range is a code: one to seven steps of the strategy's grid (the ends of its cells) after
the last point used, seven steps with no cut, one of the strategy's three cuts off the grid, or no further cut. The
search outside the kernel joins consecutive cells that one test evaluates, and consecutive dropped cells while the
joined cell's cost stays within $0.2\%$ of the sum of theirs; so the cells of the certificate are unions of the
strategy's, and may be wider than $0.004$.

The kernel searches for nothing: it reads each configuration, computes each floor from its four bits and
the box and each cut from its code, and evaluates the tests and the costs.

Lemma~\ref{lem:search} holds whatever the hints: each branch of the search led by hints is one of the nodes among which
the search chooses, with whatever configuration, floor and cut the hints give, applied only after its condition has
passed, and a cut at any point is a node (S). A hint that names a node whose condition fails makes the value the
limit, which says nothing. So the hints are not trusted: a wrong hint can make the check fail, not make it wrong.

\subsection{The certificate at \texorpdfstring{$43/20$}{43/20}}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:certificate}
% Lean: Linnik21500.tree21500 (the bound of the first term), Linnik21500.linnik_21500 (the root's test, by evaluation), Linnik21500.part21500, Linnik21500.sound21500, Linnik21500.soundF21500, SieveAd.Plain.fillTests_sound, SieveAd.Plain.fillTestsF_eq, SieveAd.Plain.frozTestsC_okI, SieveAd.Plain.frozTestsC_okJ, SieveAd.Plain.rules_frozC, SieveAd.pr_buch, SieveAd.pr_split, SieveAd.pr_mono
Let $L = 43/20$ and $f_* = 49170839016$. The box of no prime passes (J3) at $f_*$ with $\mathcal S_0 = \mathcal S_1 =
\emptyset$ and $\iota = 1$, and the term of the box $((f_*, D/2 + 1))$ with the sign $-$ is bounded at $c/\mathcal K$,
by the search of this section on the parts of the certificate and the steps of Buchstab's identity that join them,
with
\[
  c = 1093510193343867034 = 0.94846890\ldots\, \mathcal K < 0.94847\, \mathcal K.
\]
\end{proposition}

The proposition is a computation, which the kernel carries out (Section~\ref{sec:lean}); with Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} it
gives Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} (Section~\ref{sec:proof}).

The floor $f_*$, with $f_*/D = 0.0447206\ldots$, is close to the limit above which the admissible range of (J3) is no
wider than the floor, about $(L - 2)/((2 - \lambda)L) = 0.0448$. The floor $f$ raises the largest rest $r_2 = r_1 + f
+ 1$ of (J3), and each unit of $f$ lowers the upper end $v$ of the admissible range by $1 - \lambda$, while the lower
end $u$ depends on $r_1$ alone. At the root, $r_1 = 255700380$, and the admissible range $[u, v]$ of (J3)
(Definition~\ref{def:tests}) has $u = 511328600504$ and $v = 560499439521$, that is $[0.4650507, 0.5097713]$ in
exponents of $x$: $f_* = v - u - 1$ is the largest floor with $u + f_* < v$, and $(\mathrm L)$, with $a_1 = (u +
r_2)/D$ and $a_2 = 0$, reads $(u + r_2)/D = 0.5100038\ldots < 1 - 1/L = 0.5348837\ldots$.

The first prime's range $[f_*, D/2 + 1)$ is cut into $104$ pieces. Three are parts of the certificate, which cover
$[f_*, 0.1007)$ and are searched with the limit $\mathcal K$ and led by hints. The other $101$ are cells, $99$ of side
$4398046511/D$ from $110743490170/D = 0.10072\ldots$ and two up to $D/2 + 1$; each has its own step of Buchstab's
identity, taken outside the parts, and the next prime's range is cut again into parts and further cells. In all $256$
cells are cut
so, $101$ of the first prime and $155$ of the second; $227$ of their steps of Buchstab's identity use (J3), $29$ use
(J2), and $217$ are at the floor $f_*$. There are $1552$ parts of the certificate, in $17$ files.
Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(a) and~(b) join the pieces, and their costs add up to $c$ exactly. The pieces' costs
show where the cost goes:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
\toprule
$\log p_1/\log x$ & cost$/\mathcal K$ \\
\midrule
$[0.0447, 0.1007)$ & $0.0009$ \\
$[0.1007, 0.2007)$ & $0.0154$ \\
$[0.2007, 0.3007)$ & $0.1383$ \\
$[0.3007, 0.4007)$ & $0.4042$ \\
$[0.4007, 0.5]$ & $0.3897$ \\
\midrule
all & $0.9485$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
More than four fifths of the cost is in the cells of the first prime above $x^{0.3}$, and the most expensive cells of
side
$0.004$ cost about $0.02\mathcal K$ each, near $p_1 = x^{0.41}$ and $x^{0.49}$; below $x^{0.2}$ the first prime costs
less than $0.02\mathcal K$ in all.

The kernel's search takes $3400745$ nodes: $3400489$ in the parts of the certificate and the $256$ steps of
Buchstab's identity outside them; $161674$ of them are reversals through the largest prime. The hints take $5220964$
bytes. The search outside the kernel that made the plan visits $34356403$ boxes, $28257594$ of them over frozen parts,
in about nine minutes on four cores. Section~\ref{sec:lean} gives the kernel's time, and \S S5 and \S S6 the
statistics in more detail.

\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}}\label{sec:proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}]
Take $L = 43/20$. Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}, under Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, is the Type~II estimate in two variables
on the region $\mathcal R_L$, so Harman's comparisons of Section~\ref{sec:type1} hold at $L$, and with them the tests of
Section~\ref{sec:decomp}. Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate} gives a floor $f_*$ at which the box of no prime passes
(J3), and bounds the term of the root box $\mathfrak b_0$ with the sign $-$ at $c/\mathcal K$, with $c < 0.94847\,
\mathcal K$. Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} gives Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} under the input.
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} enters through Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum} and the top bin in Section~\ref{sec:type2},
through the kernel bound of Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds} (where the Pólya--Vinogradov inequality would do instead),
through the piece of $g_{\mathrm H}$ above $11/12$, which only makes $g_{\mathrm H}$ a density curve on all of
$[1/2, 1]$, and, in the formalisation, through the prime number theorem, which it derives from the case of $\zeta$
in the form $\psi(t) - t = O(t^{11/12 + \varepsilon})$ and uses for the main term of Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} and for the
sums over primes of Sections~\ref{sec:type1} and~\ref{sec:decomp}.
\end{proof}

The proof does not compute $K$: the threshold $q_0$ comes from limits in $q$ in each step, and the small moduli
from Dirichlet's theorem. The paper does not claim that its constants are effective. Its thresholds are in any case
enormous: the cells of exponents are multiples of $1/D = 2^{-40}$, and the steps of Section~\ref{sec:decomp} ask,
among other things, $x^{1/D} \ge 3$, that is $q \ge 3^{D/L}$. The argument needs no step on an exceptional zero,
since Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} leaves none.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:count} at $43/20$]
Take $L = 43/20$ and
\[
  C = \frac{1093510193343867034}{\mathcal K},
\]
the cost of Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate} over $\mathcal K$. As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320},
Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} applies with this $C$, and its count (b) is part (b) of the corollary. With $c_0 = (1 - C)/2$
and the $q_0$ of (b), Lemma~\ref{lem:every-x} gives part (a) of the corollary, with $c = (1 - C)/2^{L + 2}$.
\end{proof}

\section{The resonant window}\label{sec:window}

Sections~\ref{sec:type2} to~\ref{sec:proof} bound the prime sums of a rest only through the zeros: in the bin $i$ each
prime sum of length $Q$ is at most about $Q^{\beta_i}$, and the bin has at most about $q^{g(\beta_i)}$ characters. In
two variables the bin's size multiplies only the kernel bound $\mathcal H$ (Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds}), and this
suffices on the region $\mathcal R_L$. In one variable Cauchy--Schwarz is taken over the bin, so the bin's size enters
orthogonality as $q^{g(\beta_i)/2}$. For a polynomial of length about $q$ the estimate then needs $L \ge 1 + A/2$,
with $A$ the largest
exponent of a density estimate on all of $[1/2, 11/12]$ (the remark at the end of Section~\ref{sec:type2}): $11/5$
with Huxley's $12/5$ and $13/6$ with Chen, Gupta and Li's $7/3$, both above $21/10$. So a term of the sieve whose rest
is counted only through the zeros' bins is evaluated in one variable only where the largest density exponent allows it.

The estimate need not rely only on the number of characters that have a zero. It can also use the number of
characters at which the rest's own prime sums are large, and those are polynomials of known lengths. The $k$-th power
of a prime sum of length
$Q = q^s$ is a Dirichlet polynomial of length about $Q^k$ whose coefficients are bounded in terms of $k$; its mean
value over the characters and Input~\ref{inp:cgl} count the characters at which it is large. The sieves for primes in
short intervals count their own polynomials in the same way \cite{hb-iwaniec, bhp, harman}.

This section proves an estimate that uses these counts, and gives its tests; together they are called the
\emph{resonant window}. Section~\ref{sec:window-estimate} counts the characters at which a prime sum is large, sorts
the characters of a bin into classes by the levels of the rest's prime sums, and proves the estimate in one variable
(Theorem~\ref{thm:window}) under a condition on the exponents of every class.
Section~\ref{sec:window-terms} carries it to the sieve's terms, Section~\ref{sec:corners} ties the variable to the
rest on a box, and Section~\ref{sec:window-tests} gives the tests that Lean's kernel checks in the certificate.

A heuristic remark, which the paper does not use, shows what the counts give; the paragraph after the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:window} compares it with the theorem. Let $W$ have length $P \ge q$ and coefficients of modulus at
most $1$, let the rest have the prime sums
$S_{Q_j}$, $Q_j = q^{s_j}$, with $P Q_1 \cdots Q_K = q^L$, and let $k_j$ be the least integer with $k_j s_j \ge 1$. If
$\sum_j 1/k_j \ge 1$, take $a_j \in [0, 1]$ with $\sum_j a_j/k_j = 1$, and write $|S_{Q_j}| \le |S_{Q_j}|^{a_j} U_j^{1 -
a_j}$, where $U_j \approx Q_j^{11/12 + \varepsilon}$, for a small $\varepsilon > 0$, is the bound that
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} gives through Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum}. By H\"older's inequality with the exponents $2$ and
$2k_j/a_j$, the sum over $\chi \ne \chi_0$ of $|W(\chi)| \prod_j |S_{Q_j}(\chi)|$ is at most $(\sum_\chi |W|^2)^{1/2}
\prod_j (\sum_\chi |S_{Q_j}|^{2k_j})^{a_j/(2k_j)} U_j^{1 - a_j}$. Orthogonality gives $\sum_\chi |W|^2 \ll P^2$ and,
since $Q_j^{k_j} \ge q$, $\sum_\chi |S_{Q_j}|^{2k_j} \ll Q_j^{2k_j}$. So the sum is $\ll q^L \prod_j Q_j^{-(1/12 -
\varepsilon)(1 - a_j)}$. As $\sum_j s_j(1 - a_j) \ge \sum_j (1 - a_j)/k_j = \sum_j 1/k_j - 1$, the sum is $\ll q^{L -
\kappa}$ with $\kappa = (1/12 - \varepsilon)(\sum_j 1/k_j - 1)$, a saving when $\sum_j 1/k_j > 1$.

A rest of one prime never gives a saving, nor a rest of two primes both below $q$; two primes above $q^{1/2}$ and any
third prime give one, and so do primes above $q^{1/2}$, $q^{1/3}$ and $q^{1/5}$.

\subsection{The estimate}\label{sec:window-estimate}

Throughout, $q > 1$, $S_Q(\chi, h)$ is the smoothed prime sum of Section~\ref{sec:type2}, and
\[
  W(\chi) = \sum_{n \in S} a_n \chi(n) n^{-ih_0}, \qquad S \subseteq (P, 2P], \qquad V = \sum_{n \in S} |a_n|^2,
\]
is \emph{the variable}, of length $P = q^m$. The \emph{rest} is a product of prime sums $S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)$, $1 \le j
\le K$, with $Q_j = q^{s_j}$. Exponents are exponents of $q$, except the sizes $t_j$ below, which are exponents of
$Q_j$.

\subsubsection*{The characters at which a prime sum is large}

For an integer $k \ge 1$ and reals $s$ and $u$ let
\[
  \mathfrak m_k(s, u) = \max(1, ks) + ks(1 - 2u).
\]
The prime sum $S_Q$ of Section~\ref{sec:type2}, given there for $Q \ge 4$, is defined by the same formula for every
$Q \ge 1$.

\begin{lemma}[the moments]\label{lem:moments}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.card_Sp_large, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_chars_Sp_pow_le, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_chars_dp_pow_le, SieveTypeIIHM.card_large_le, SieveTypeIIHM.card_tuples_primes_le, SieveTypeIIHM.momExp
Let $q > 1$, let $F_0$ bound $|f_0|$, let $Q \ge 1$, let $h, u \in \mathbb R$ and let $k \ge 1$ be an integer. With $s
= \log_q Q$,
\[
  \#\{\chi \bmod q : |S_Q(\chi, h)| \ge Q^u\} \le (2^k + 1)\, k^k (3F_0^2)^k\, q^{\mathfrak m_k(s, u)}.
\]
\end{lemma}

The count is over all the characters mod $q$, the trivial one included. The proof bounds the $2k$-th moment of the
prime sum by orthogonality on its $k$-th power and applies Markov's inequality.

\begin{proof}
The prime sum is a Dirichlet polynomial $\sum_{p \le 2Q} c_p \chi(p)$ over the primes up to $2Q$, with $c_p =
(p/Q)^{-ih} f_0(p/Q)$, so $|c_p| \le F_0$ and $\sum_p |c_p|^2 \le F_0^2(2Q + 1) \le 3F_0^2 Q$. Its $k$-th power is
$\sum_n c^{(k)}_n \chi(n)$, where $c^{(k)}_n$ is the sum of $c_{p_1} \cdots c_{p_k}$ over the $k$-tuples of primes $p_i
\le 2Q$ with $p_1 \cdots p_k = n$, and $n \le (2Q)^k$. A tuple with the product $n$ has its entries among the prime
factors of any one such tuple, of which there are at most $k$; so there are at most $k^k$ such tuples, and by
Cauchy--Schwarz $\sum_n |c^{(k)}_n|^2 \le k^k (\sum_p |c_p|^2)^k$. Orthogonality on the interval $(0, (2Q)^k]$, as in
Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds} (each residue class mod $q$ has at most $(2Q)^k/q + 1$ elements in it), gives
\[
  \sum_{\chi \bmod q} |S_Q(\chi, h)|^{2k} \le \bigl((2Q)^k + q\bigr)\, k^k (3F_0^2 Q)^k.
\]
The characters with $|S_Q(\chi, h)| \ge Q^u$ contribute at least $Q^{2ku}$ each to the left side. With $Q = q^s$, $s
\ge 0$, and $(2Q)^k + q \le (2^k + 1) q^{\max(1, ks)}$, their number is at most $(2^k + 1) k^k (3F_0^2)^k$ times $q$ to
the power $\max(1, ks) + ks - 2ksu = \mathfrak m_k(s, u)$.
\end{proof}

For $ks \ge 1$ the exponent is $2ks(1 - u)$; where $Q^k$ is about $q$ ($ks = 1$) it is $2(1 - u)$, the count that a
density exponent $2$ would give at $\sigma = u$. The second count is by Input~\ref{inp:cgl}, applied to the $k$-th
power. Let $\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} = 10^{-5}$
and
\[
  \mathfrak r_k(s, u) = \max\Bigl(2ks(1 - u),\ \frac12 + ks(3 - 4u),\ \frac43 + ks(2 - 4u)\Bigr),
\]
the exponents of the three terms of Input~\ref{inp:cgl} for a sum of length $q^{ks}$ at the threshold $q^{ksu}$, at $T
= 1$.
Let $\mathfrak v_k(s, u) = 1$, except where $ks \ge 2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$ and $u \ge 7/10 +
\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$; there
\[
  \mathfrak v_k(s, u) = \min\Bigl(1,\ \mathfrak r_k\bigl(s, \min(u, 4/5 - \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}})\bigr) +
  \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}
  \Bigr).
\]
So $0 \le \mathfrak v_k(s, u) \le 1$ for $s \ge 0$, $\mathfrak v_k(s, u)$ does not increase in $u$, and it is $1$ for
$u < 7/10 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$ and for $ks \ge 5/2$. At $u = 3/4$ and $5/6
\le ks \le 1$ it is $1/2 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$, where the moment gives $1 - ks/2$, which is larger for $ks < 1 -
2\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$: there Input~\ref{inp:cgl} counts fewer characters.

\begin{lemma}[the large values of the powers]\label{lem:lv-powers}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.card_Sp_large_lv, SieveTypeIIHM.card_Sp_pow_large, SieveTypeIIHM.lvExp, SieveTypeIIHM.lvRaw, LinnikCGLLV.cgl_all, LinnikCGLLV.lv_range
Assume Input~\ref{inp:cgl}. Let $k_{\max} \ge 1$ be an integer and $F_1 \ge 1$. There are $C \ge 1$ and $q_0$ such that
for every $q \ge q_0$, every real function $f$ with $|f| \le F_1$ and support in $[1/2, 2]$, all reals $Q \ge 4$,
$h$ and $u$, and every integer $1 \le k \le k_{\max}$, the prime sum $S_Q(\chi, h)$ formed with $f$ in place of $f_0$
satisfies
\[
  \#\{\chi \bmod q : |S_Q(\chi, h)| \ge Q^u\} \le C\, q^{\mathfrak v_k(\log_q Q,\, u)}.
\]
\end{lemma}

The proof applies Input~\ref{inp:cgl} at $T = 1$, at the point $0$ for every character, to the dyadic blocks of the
$k$-th power, and then compares exponents.

\begin{proof}
\emph{All characters.} The proof uses Input~\ref{inp:cgl} in the form for all the characters mod $q$ stated after it,
at $T = 1$.

\emph{The power.} As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:moments}, $S_Q(\chi, h)^k = \sum_n c^{(k)}_n \chi(n)$ with
$|c^{(k)}_n| \le k^k F_1^k$, and $c^{(k)}_n = 0$ outside $[(Q/2)^k, (2Q)^k]$ since $f$ vanishes outside $[1/2, 2]$.
With $N_0 = \lceil (Q/2)^k \rceil - 1$, the $2k + 1$ dyadic blocks $(N_02^i, N_02^{i+1}]$, $0 \le i \le 2k$, cover that
interval. If $|S_Q(\chi, h)|^k \ge Z$, one block, with the coefficients divided by $k^k F_1^k$, is at least $Z/B_k$ at
$\chi$, $B_k = (2k + 1) k^k F_1^k$. The count for all characters at $T = 1$ and $t_\chi = 0$, for each block,
applies when $(Q/2)^k \ge 2q^{2/3} + 2$ and $((2Q)^k)^{7/10} B_k \le Z \le ((Q/2)^k/2)^{4/5}$, and bounds the
characters with $|S_Q(\chi, h)|^k \ge Z$ by $(2k + 1) C q^\varepsilon (q(2Q)^k)^\varepsilon$ times its sum of three
terms at the length $(2Q)^k$ and the threshold $Z/B_k$.

\emph{The exponents.} Let $s = \log_q Q$. If $ks < 2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$, or $u < 7/10 +
\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$, or $\mathfrak r_k(s, u') + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} \ge 1$ with $u' = \min(u, 4/5 -
\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}})$, the count $\varphi(q) \le q$ suffices. Otherwise $2ks(1 - u') < 1$ gives $ks \le 5/2$, and
the previous step applies at $Z = q^{ksu'} \le |S_Q(\chi, h)|^k$ with $\varepsilon = \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/5$: the
margins $ks(u' - 7/10) \ge \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/2$ and $ks(4/5 - u') \ge \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/2$ absorb the powers
of $2$ and $B_k \le B_{k_{\max}}$ once $q^{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/2} \ge 4 \cdot 2^{k_{\max}} B_{k_{\max}}$ (which
also gives the length condition, $(Q/2)^k \ge 2^{-k_{\max}} q^{2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}} \ge 2q^{2/3} + 2$), and
$q^\varepsilon (q(2Q)^k)^\varepsilon \le 2^k q^{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}}$. Each of the three terms is
then a constant times $q$ to a term of $\mathfrak r_k(s, u')$, so the count is at most a constant, depending on
$k_{\max}$ and $F_1$ only, times $q^{\mathfrak r_k(s, u') + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}}$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection*{The classes}

Let $\tau = 10^{-6}$. The \emph{level} of the $j$-th prime sum at $\chi$ is
\[
  \ell_j(\chi) = \min\bigl(10^6,\ \lfloor \tau^{-1} \log_{Q_j} |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \rfloor\bigr),
\]
the integer part taken as $0$ where the logarithm is negative and where the sum vanishes. If $\ell_j(\chi) \ge 1$ then
$|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \ge Q_j^{\tau\ell_j(\chi)}$, and if $\ell_j(\chi) < 10^6$ then $|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le
Q_j^{(\ell_j(\chi) + 1)\tau}$. The \emph{class} of $\chi \in \mathcal X^*$ is its bin $i$ (Section~\ref{sec:bins})
together with the levels of its prime sums, $(i, \ell_1(\chi), \dots, \ell_K(\chi))$; there are at most $I (10^6 +
1)^K$ classes, with $I = 500000$ the number of bins.

On a class each prime sum is at most $2Q_j^{t_j}$, with the \emph{size} $t_j$ the smaller of the zeros' bound
$\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta$ of Section~\ref{sec:bins} and, if $\ell_j < 10^6$, the bound $(\ell_j + 1)\tau$ of the level.
The class has at most about $q^\gamma$ characters, with the \emph{count exponent} $\gamma$ the least of
the density curve's exponent at the bin and the exponents of Lemmas~\ref{lem:moments} and~\ref{lem:lv-powers} at the
levels. A class is bounded as a bin is in Section~\ref{sec:zone}, by orthogonality or by the Hal\'asz--Montgomery
inequality, but in one variable: Cauchy--Schwarz is taken over the class, and the inequality is used in its first-moment
form, $(\sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)|)^2 \le eV(2P|\mathcal X| + |\mathcal X|^2 \mathcal H)$, which follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq} (the remark after it). With $\mathcal X$ of about $q^\gamma$ characters, the rest at most about
$q^E$ on it, and $V^{1/2} = q^{m/2}(V/P)^{1/2}$, the three bounds (orthogonality, and the two terms of this
inequality) have the exponents of $q$ below.

\begin{definition}\label{def:fit}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResBinOK
Let $L$, $E$, $\gamma$ and $m$ be real, and let $b(m) = 5/12 + m/12 + \delta'(1 - m) + 2\varepsilon'$, $\delta' =
\varepsilon' = 1/1000$, be the exponent of the kernel bound (Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds}). The exponents $(E,
\gamma, m)$ \emph{fit} at $L$ if
\[
  E + \frac\gamma2 + \frac{m + \max(m, 1)}2 \le L - \frac1{25000},
\]
or both
\[
  E + \frac\gamma2 + m \le L - \frac1{25000} \qquad \text{and} \qquad E + \frac m2 + \gamma + \frac{b(m)}2 \le L -
  \frac1{25000}.
\]
\end{definition}

The first is orthogonality, $|\mathcal X|^{1/2}((P + q)V)^{1/2}$; the other two are the diagonal term $2P|\mathcal X|$
and the off-diagonal term $|\mathcal X|^2 \mathcal H$ of the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality, each of which must pass.
For $m \ge 1$ the first of the two is the bound by orthogonality, so the inequality helps only for a variable shorter
than $q$. The resonance condition (Definition~\ref{def:res}) asks this of every class that the counts allow. In it a
tuple stands for a class: $\beta = \beta_i$ for its bin $i$, $\gamma$ for its count exponent and $t_j$ for its sizes,
with the counts read one step $\tau$ lower, at $t_j - \tau$.

\begin{definition}\label{def:res}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResCond, SieveCurveHx.ghx
Let $L$ and $m$ be real, $K \ge 0$ an integer, $s_1, \dots, s_K$ real, and $\tau = 10^{-6}$. An \emph{admissible tuple}
at $(s_1, \dots, s_K)$ is a tuple $(\beta, \gamma, t_1, \dots, t_K)$ of reals with $1/2 \le \beta \le 11/12$, $\gamma
\le
g_{\mathrm H}(\beta)$ and, for $1 \le j \le K$,
\begin{gather*}
  t_j \le \beta + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}, \\
  \gamma \le \mathfrak m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) \quad \text{and} \quad \gamma \le \mathfrak v_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) \qquad
  \text{for every integer } k \ge 1.
\end{gather*}
The \emph{resonance condition} $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds if for every admissible tuple at $(s_1,
\dots, s_K)$ the exponents
\[
  \Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^K s_j(t_j - 1) + L - m,\ \gamma,\ m\Bigr)
\]
fit at $L$.
\end{definition}

The first exponent is that of $\prod_j Q_j^{t_j} = q^{\sum_j s_j(t_j - 1)} \prod_j Q_j$ when $\prod_j Q_j \le q^{L -
m}$. The bound $t_j \le \beta + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$ is the zeros' bound in the bin of $\beta$, rounded up: there $t_j \le
\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta = \beta + \varpi$, and $\varpi < 2.9 \cdot 10^{-6}$ (Section~\ref{sec:bins}) is rounded up
to $4 \cdot 10^{-6}$. The left sides of Definition~\ref{def:fit} increase with $\gamma$ and with each $t_j$, while
the hypotheses bound $\gamma$ by functions of $t_j$ that do not increase. So the condition weighs the size of a prime
sum against the number of characters at which
it can be that large, and the classes that bind combine a large size with as many characters as the counts allow at
that size. The condition reads $m$ and each $s_j$, not only their sum, and the variable may be shorter than $q$.

\begin{lemma}[one class]\label{lem:class}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.bin_bound_exp, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_typeII_cls, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_bin_hm, SieveTypeII.sum_chars_dp_sq_le
Let $L$, $E$ and $m$ be real, $\gamma \le 1$, and let $(E, \gamma, m)$ fit at $L$. Let $C_0, C_d, C_K \ge 0$ and
\[
  C' = e^{1/2}\bigl(2\sqrt3\, C_0 C_d^{1/2} + 6 C_0 C_d C_K^{1/2}\bigr).
\]
Let $q > 1$, $P = q^m$ an integer, $1 \le T \le 3q^\eta$ with $\eta \ge 0$, and let $0 \le \mathcal H \le C_K (2P)^{1/12
-
\delta'} (2q)^{5/12 + \delta' + 2\varepsilon'}$ bound $|k_{2P}(\psi)|$ for every non-trivial character $\psi$ mod $q$.
Let $\mathcal X$ be a set of at most $C_d (qT)^\gamma (1 + \log qT)^{10}$ characters on which $\prod_{j=1}^K
|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le C_0 q^E$, for some $K$, $Q_j$ and $h_j$. Then for all $S \subseteq (P, 2P]$, coefficients and
$h_0$,
\[
  \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)| \prod_{j=1}^K |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le C' (1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{L - 1/25000 +
  \eta} \Bigl(\frac VP\Bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
\end{lemma}

The proof is that of Lemma~\ref{lem:bin}, with one polynomial: the count of the class enters both bounds.

\begin{proof}
The left side is at most $C_0 q^E \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)|$. By Cauchy--Schwarz over $\mathcal X$ and
orthogonality over all the characters, $\sum_{\mathcal X} |W| \le |\mathcal X|^{1/2}((P + q)V)^{1/2}$; by the
first-moment form of Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq}, $\sum_{\mathcal X} |W| \le (eV(2P|\mathcal X| + |\mathcal X|^2
\mathcal H))^{1/2}$. Since $\gamma \le 1$, $1 \le T \le 3q^\eta$ give $(qT)^{\gamma/2} \le \sqrt3\, q^{\gamma/2 +
\eta/2}$ and $(qT)^\gamma \le 3 q^{\gamma + \eta}$, whatever the sign of $\gamma$.

If $(E, \gamma, m)$ fit by orthogonality, use the first bound: $(P + q)P \le 2q^{m + \max(m, 1)}$, so the left side is
at most $\sqrt6\, C_0 C_d^{1/2} (1 + \log qT)^5 q^{E + \gamma/2 + (m + \max(m, 1))/2 + \eta/2} (V/P)^{1/2}$, and the
exponent is at most $L - 1/25000 + \eta/2$.

Otherwise use the second:
\[
  \bigl(eV(2P|\mathcal X| + |\mathcal X|^2\mathcal H)\bigr)^{1/2} \le e^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac VP\Bigr)^{1/2}\bigl(\sqrt2\,
  |\mathcal X|^{1/2} P + P^{1/2} |\mathcal X| \mathcal H^{1/2}\bigr).
\]
The first term gives at most $\sqrt6\, e^{1/2} C_0 C_d^{1/2} (1 + \log qT)^5 (V/P)^{1/2}$ times $q$ to the power $E +
\gamma/2 + m + \eta/2$. For the second, $2^{1/12 - \delta'} 2^{5/12 + \delta' + 2\varepsilon'} \le 4$, so $\mathcal H
\le 4C_K q^{b(m)}$, and it gives at most $6 e^{1/2} C_0 C_d C_K^{1/2} (1 + \log qT)^{10} (V/P)^{1/2}$ times $q$ to the
power $E + m/2 + \gamma + b(m)/2 + \eta$. The two inequalities of Definition~\ref{def:fit} bound the exponents by $L -
1/25000 + \eta/2$ and $L - 1/25000 + \eta$. In both cases $(1 + \log qT)^5 \le (1 + \log qT)^{10}$ and $q^{\eta/2} \le
q^\eta$, and the constants are at most $C'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[the resonant window]\label{thm:window}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.typeII_res, SieveCurveHx.counts_hx, SieveCurveHx.ghx_le_one, SieveCurveHx.ghx_half
Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}. Let $L$ be real, let $K_0 \ge 0$ be an integer, $e_1 > 0$ and $0
< \eta \le 10^{-5}$. There is $q_0$, depending on $f_0$, $K_0$, $e_1$, $\eta$ and $L$, such that for every $q \ge q_0$,
every integer $P \ge 1$, every $S \subseteq (P, 2P]$, all coefficients $a_n$ and $h_0 \in \mathbb R$, every $K \le
K_0$, and all $Q_1, \dots, Q_K \ge q^{e_1}$ and $|h_1|, \dots, |h_K| \le q^\eta$ with $Q_1 \cdots Q_K \le 2^{K_0}
q^L/P$, if $\operatorname{Res}_L(\log_q P; \log_q Q_1, \dots, \log_q Q_K)$ holds, then
\[
  \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X^*} |W(\chi)| \prod_{j=1}^K |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le q^{L - 1/50000} \Bigl(\frac VP
  \Bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
\end{theorem}

As after Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}, the trivial character's term is at most a constant times $q^L(V/P)^{1/2}$, and the
bound saves $q^{-1/50000}$ against it. The proof is that of Proposition~\ref{prop:type2-gen}, with the classes in place
of the bins: each class satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:class} at exponents that the condition makes fit.
Of the curve $g_{\mathrm H}$ it uses only that it is a density curve (Proposition~\ref{prop:curve}) with $g_{\mathrm H}
\le 1$ and $g_{\mathrm H}(1/2) = 1$ (for the bin $0$, Section~\ref{sec:bins}).

\begin{proof}
Take $\delta = 10^{-6}$, $H = q^\eta$, $T = 2H + 1 \le 3q^\eta$ and the bins of Section~\ref{sec:bins}. Write $m =
\log_q P$ and $s_j = \log_q Q_j \ge e_1$. As there, for large $q$ each $Q_j \ge 4$ and $Q_j \le q^{1 + L}$, and in the
bin $i$ every $|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le 2Q_j^{\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta}$. Let $F_0$ bound $|f_0|$, $k_1 = \lceil 1/e_1
\rceil + 1$, $k_2 = \lceil 5/(2e_1) \rceil + 1$, and let $C_{\mathrm{lv}}$ be the constant of
Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers} at $k_{\max} = k_2$ and $F_1 = \max(F_0, 1)$, for $q$ beyond the lemma's $q_0$; let $C_d$ be
the largest of $C_{\mathrm{lv}}$, $1$, the constant of the density curve $g_{\mathrm H}$, and the constants of
Lemma~\ref{lem:moments} for $1 \le k \le k_1$.

Fix a class $c = (i, \ell_1, \dots, \ell_K)$, and let $t_j = \min(\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta, (\ell_j + 1)\tau)$ if
$\ell_j < 10^6$ and $t_j = \beta_{i+1} + 2\delta$ if $\ell_j = 10^6$. Then $t_j \le \beta_i + 1/1200000 + 2 \cdot
10^{-6} \le \beta_i + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$.

\emph{The rest.} On the class, $|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le 2Q_j^{t_j}$, by the bin and by the level of the prime sum. So
the product is
at most $2^K q^{\sum_j s_j t_j} = 2^K q^{\sum_j s_j(t_j - 1)} \prod_j Q_j \le 4^{K_0} q^E$, with $E = \sum_j s_j(t_j -
1) + L - m$, since $\prod_j Q_j \le 2^{K_0} q^{L - m}$.

\emph{The count.} Let $\gamma$ be the least of $g_{\mathrm H}(\beta_i)$, of $\mathfrak m_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j)$ for $1 \le
k \le k_1$, and of $\mathfrak v_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j)$ for $1 \le k \le k_2$, over the $j$ with $\ell_j \ge 1$. The class
lies in the set counted by the least of these: the bin $i$, with at most $C_d (qT)^{g_{\mathrm H}(\beta_i)} (1 + \log
qT)^{10}$ characters (Section~\ref{sec:bins}), or, for a $j$ with $\ell_j \ge 1$, the characters with $|S_{Q_j}(\chi,
h_j)| \ge Q_j^{\tau\ell_j}$, counted by Lemma~\ref{lem:moments} or Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers}. In the last two cases
$\gamma \ge 0$, since $s_j \ge 0$ and $\tau\ell_j \le 1$, so $q^\gamma \le (qT)^\gamma$. In every case the class has
at most $C_d (qT)^\gamma (1 + \log qT)^{10}$ characters, and $\gamma \le g_{\mathrm H}(\beta_i) \le 1$. The counts are
not combined: the class is counted by the single least of them.

\emph{The condition.} The tuple $(\beta_i, \gamma, t_1, \dots, t_K)$ has $1/2 \le \beta_i \le 11/12$, $\gamma \le
g_{\mathrm H}(\beta_i)$ and $t_j \le \beta_i + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$. For it to be an admissible tuple at $(s_1, \dots,
s_K)$ (Definition~\ref{def:res}) it remains to see that $\gamma \le \mathfrak m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau)$ and $\gamma
\le \mathfrak v_k(s_j, t_j - \tau)$ for every $j$ and every $k \ge 1$. If $\ell_j = 0$, then $t_j - \tau \le 0$, so
$\mathfrak m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) \ge 1 \ge \gamma$ and $\mathfrak v_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) = 1$. If $\ell_j \ge 1$, then
$t_j - \tau \le \tau\ell_j$ (at the cap because $\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta - \tau \le 1$), and $\mathfrak m_k$ and
$\mathfrak v_k$ do not increase in their second argument, so it suffices that $\gamma \le \mathfrak m_k(s_j,
\tau\ell_j)$ and $\gamma \le \mathfrak v_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j)$. For $k \le k_1$ and $k \le k_2$ this is the choice of
$\gamma$. For $k > k_1$, $k_0 = \lceil 1/s_j \rceil \le \lceil 1/e_1 \rceil < k_1$ has $k_0 s_j \ge 1$, and for $u \le
1$, $\mathfrak m_k(s, u) = 2ks(1 - u)$ does not decrease in $k \ge k_0$, so $\mathfrak m_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j) \ge
\mathfrak m_{k_0}(s_j, \tau\ell_j) \ge \gamma$. For $k > k_2$, $ks_j \ge ke_1 \ge 5/2$, so $\mathfrak v_k(s_j,
\tau\ell_j) = 1 \ge \gamma$. So the tuple is admissible, and by $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ the
exponents $(E, \gamma, m)$ fit at $L$.

\emph{The sum.} The kernel bound of Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds} gives $\mathcal H$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:class}, with a
constant $C_K$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:class} with $C_0 = 4^{K_0}$ and this $C_d$, applied to the characters of $\mathcal
X^*$ in the class, each class contributes at most $C'(1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{L - 1/25000 + \eta}(V/P)^{1/2}$, with $C'$
independent of $q$. There are at most $I(10^6 + 1)^{K_0}$ classes, and $1 + \log qT \le 3(1 + \log q)$. As in the proof
of Proposition~\ref{prop:type2-gen}, for large $q$ the number of classes times $C'(1 + \log qT)^{10}$ is at most
$q^{1/100000}$, and $L - 1/25000 + \eta + 1/100000 \le L - 1/50000$ since $\eta \le 10^{-5}$.
\end{proof}

The heuristic remark at the beginning of the section and Theorem~\ref{thm:window} are related as follows. On a class,
the moment with the power $k_j$ gives $\gamma \le \mathfrak m_{k_j}(s_j, t_j - \tau) = 2k_js_j(1 - t_j + \tau)$, that
is $s_j(1 - t_j) \ge \gamma/(2k_j) - \tau s_j$, and the half-plane gives $s_j(1 - t_j) \ge s_j(1/12 - 4 \cdot
10^{-6})$. Taking $a_j$ times the first bound plus $1 - a_j$ times the second, with the $a_j$ of the remark, gives the
following for $m \ge 1$ (an elementary check, not in the library and not used). The first inequality of
Definition~\ref{def:fit} holds at every admissible tuple once $(1/12 - 4 \cdot 10^{-6})(\sum_j 1/k_j - 1) \ge 1/25000
+ \tau \sum_j s_j$, for instance when $\sum_j 1/k_j \ge 1 + 6 \cdot 10^{-4}$ and $\sum_j s_j \le 5/2$. So, up to the
margins, the
rests of the remark pass, and Definition~\ref{def:res} admits more: the density curve's count where the zeros are rare,
the large values
of a power whose length $q^{ks}$ lies between $q^{2/3}$ and $q^{5/2}$, and the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality where
the variable is shorter than $q$. Which boxes of the sieve satisfy the condition is decided by the tests of
Section~\ref{sec:window-tests}.

\subsection{Terms with a rest}\label{sec:window-terms}

Theorem~\ref{thm:window} bounds the sum over $\chi \in \mathcal X^*$ of $|W(\chi)| \prod_j |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)|$, for
one Dirichlet polynomial $W(\chi)$, the variable, and the prime sums of a rest, where $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1,
\dots, s_K)$ holds (Definition~\ref{def:res}). This subsection carries it to the sieve's terms, as
Lemmas~\ref{lem:family-two} to~\ref{lem:term-two} carry Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}. The comparisons, $N_1 = \lfloor x
\rfloor$, $N_2 = \lfloor 2x \rfloor$ and $\vartheta$ are those of Section~\ref{sec:type1}, and $x = q^L$.

For a $k$-tuple $\mathbf z = (z_1, \dots, z_k)$ of primes and $K$ distinct indices $i_1, \dots, i_K$ of $\{1, \dots,
k\}$, with $\mathcal S_r = \{i_1, \dots, i_K\}$, the \emph{rest} is the primes $z_{i_1}, \dots, z_{i_K}$, and the
\emph{variable} is the product $\prod_{t \notin \mathcal S_r} z_t$ of the other entries. Each prime of the rest gives a
prime sum of its own, at its dyadic scale $Q_j$, and the variable gives the polynomial $W$ over the values of the
product. For a term $\sum_{\mathbf r} (S(\mathcal A_r, r_k) - S(\mathcal B_r, r_k)/\varphi(q))$ over decreasing tuples
of primes $r_1 > \dots > r_k$, $r = r_1 \cdots r_k$, the rest is a set of the tuple's primes, and the variable is the
integer $n$ counted divided by the rest: the other primes of the tuple times the cofactor $n/r$. Two things differ from
Section~\ref{sec:type1}. There is one variable, not two, so no condition is ever between two variables. And
$\operatorname{Res}_L$ reads the exponent of each prime sum of the rest, not only their total, and it is asked only
above lower bounds $\sigma_j$ that the primes of the rest respect.

\begin{definition}\label{def:res-quadrant}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResBox
Let $L$ be real, $K \ge 1$ an integer, and $m_0, \sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_K$ reals. The condition
$\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds \emph{on the quadrant} $(m_0; \sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_K)$ if
$\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds for all reals $m, s_1, \dots, s_K$ with
\begin{gather*}
  m \ge m_0, \qquad s_j \ge \sigma_j \ (1 \le j \le K), \\
  \sum_{j = 1}^K s_j \le L, \qquad \sum_{j = 1}^K s_j + m \le L + 10^{-5}.
\end{gather*}
\end{definition}

The quadrant is bounded below by $m_0$ and the $\sigma_j$. Its only upper bounds, $\sum_j s_j \le L$ and $\sum_j s_j +
m \le L + 10^{-5}$, are those that the dyadic scales of a family give: the rest and the variable make up $x = q^L$,
up to a bounded power of $2$, at most $q^{10^{-5}}$.

\begin{definition}\label{def:tuple-estimate}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.FullEst
Let $L$ and $e_0$ be reals, $K \ge 1$ an integer, and $\mathcal G = (\mathcal G_q)_{q \ge 1}$ sets of $K$-tuples of
non-negative integers, one for each $q$, a \emph{region} of the rest. The \emph{tuple estimate}
holds for $\mathcal G$ at $(L, e_0)$ if for all integers $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$ and every real $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$
there
are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$ prime to $q$. Let $i_1,
\dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\mathbf z$ run over the $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$
that satisfy a list of at most $J_0$ comparisons with constant terms at most $\Lambda_1 \log q$. If every tuple of
primes that
satisfies the comparisons has $z_t \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $t$, $(z_{i_1}, \dots, z_{i_K}) \in \mathcal G_q$ and $N_1 <
z_1 \cdots z_k \le N_2$, then
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf z} \vartheta(z_1 \cdots z_k)\Bigr| \le \frac{q^{L - \delta}}{\varphi(q)}.
\]
\end{definition}

In the tuple estimate $\delta$ may depend on the length $k$, as well as on $J_0$ and $\Lambda_1$; this is needed
because a term's cofactor adds primes to its tuple. It does not depend on the indices or on the comparisons
themselves, which are chosen after $q$.

\begin{proposition}[tuples of primes with a rest]\label{prop:tuples-rest}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.full_res_exp (from SieveTypeIIHM.full_res_bound, SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_core,
% SieveTypeIIHM.box_res_gen, SieveUnwGen.unw_gen_lo)
Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}. Let $0 < L \le 5/2$ and $e_0 > 0$, let $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$
be integers, and
let $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$, $\rho_2 \ge 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$ be reals with $L - \rho_2 - \varepsilon > 0$. There are
$\delta > 0$ and $q_0$, depending only on $L$, $e_0$, $k$, $J_0$, $\Lambda_1$, $\rho_2$ and $\varepsilon$, such that
the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$ prime to $q$. Let $K \ge 1$, let $i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct
indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_K$ and $\rho_1$ be reals
such that $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant $(L - \rho_2 - \varepsilon; \sigma_1 - \varepsilon, \dots,
\sigma_K - \varepsilon)$. Let $\mathbf z$ run over the $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$ that satisfy a list of at most
$J_0$ comparisons with constant terms at most $\Lambda_1 \log q$, and suppose that every tuple of primes that
satisfies the
comparisons has
\begin{gather*}
  z_t \ge q^{e_0} \ (1 \le t \le k), \qquad z_{i_j} \ge q^{\sigma_j} \ (1 \le j \le K), \\
  q^{\rho_1} \le \prod_{j = 1}^K z_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}, \qquad N_1 < z_1 \cdots z_k \le N_2.
\end{gather*}
Then
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf z} \vartheta(z_1 \cdots z_k)\Bigr| \le \frac{q^{L - \delta}}{\varphi(q)}.
\]
\end{proposition}

So, for fixed $K$, $\sigma_j$ and $\rho_1$ with $\operatorname{Res}_L$ on that quadrant, the proposition says that the
tuple estimate holds at $(L, e_0)$ for the region of the $K$-tuples $\mathbf y$ with $y_j \ge q^{\sigma_j}$ and
$q^{\rho_1} \le y_1 \cdots y_K \le q^{\rho_2}$. The margin $\varepsilon$ is spent twice, on the variable's lower end and
on the rest's dyadic scales. The lower bound $q^{\rho_1}$ is no restriction: $\rho_1 = 0$ always satisfies it, as a
product of primes is at least $1$, and $\delta$ does not depend on $\rho_1$.

The proof is in \S S7; here is an outline. It is that of Lemma~\ref{lem:chains-two} with one variable, the integer
$\prod_{t \notin \mathcal S_r} z_t$. Group the tuples of the variable by their product: at most $(k + 1)^k$ tuples have a
given product, so a polynomial over them is a polynomial over the integers with coefficients at most $(k + 1)^k$, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:window} bounds it, block by dyadic block of the variable, at each frequency of the separation of
variables. A
comparison with a nonzero coefficient at an index of $\mathcal S_r$ has a prime of the rest as a pivot. Every other
comparison is a condition on the variable's tuple alone, and goes into the set over which the polynomial runs. So
Lemma~\ref{lem:cross} is not needed: with one variable there is no condition between two variables, and the
coefficients lose a constant, not a power of $\log q$. The argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:family-sharp-two} then applies,
with one change: a dyadic piece in which the scale of a prime of the rest is below $q^{\sigma_j - \varepsilon}$ is
empty, so the condition is asked only at scales above it. The hypotheses $q^{\rho_1} \le \prod_j z_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}$
and $N_1 < z_1 \cdots z_k$ put the variable above $\lfloor N_1/\lfloor q^{\rho_2} \rfloor \rfloor \ge q^{L - \rho_2 -
\varepsilon}$, and the scales of a nonempty piece have $\sum_j s_j \le L$ and $\sum_j s_j + m \le L + 10^{-5}$ for large
$q$: the quadrant of the hypothesis.

\begin{lemma}[a term with a rest]\label{lem:term-rest}
% Lean: (a) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_gen; (b) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_exp
Let $2 < L \le 5/2$, $e_0 > 0$, let $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$ be integers, and $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$ a real.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Let $K \ge 1$ and let $\mathcal G$ be a region of $K$-tuples for which the tuple estimate holds at $(L,
  e_0)$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$ prime to
  $q$. Let $i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $(r_1, \dots, r_k)$ run over the
  $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$ that satisfy a list of at most $J_0$ comparisons with constant terms at most
  $\Lambda_1 \log q$. Suppose that every tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing, has
  $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$
  for every $i$, and has $(r_{i_1}, \dots, r_{i_K}) \in \mathcal G_q$. Then, with $r = r_1 \cdots r_k$,
  \[
    \Bigl|\sum_{(r_1, \dots, r_k)} \Bigl(S(\mathcal A_r, r_k) - \frac{S(\mathcal B_r, r_k)}{\varphi(q)}\Bigr)\Bigr|
    \le \frac{q^{L - \delta}}{\varphi(q)}.
  \]
\item[(b)] Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}, and let $\rho_2 \ge 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$ with $L -
\rho_2 -
  \varepsilon > 0$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$
  prime to $q$. Let $K \ge 1$, let $i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\sigma_1,
  \dots, \sigma_K$ and $\rho_1$ be reals such that $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant $(L - \rho_2 -
  \varepsilon; \sigma_1 - \varepsilon, \dots, \sigma_K - \varepsilon)$. Let the tuples run as in (a), and suppose that
  every tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing, has $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $i$,
  $r_{i_j} \ge q^{\sigma_j}$ for every $j$, and $q^{\rho_1} \le \prod_j r_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}$. Then the bound of (a)
  holds.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

The rest is a set of the tuple's primes; the variable is everything else of $n$, the cofactor included, and no
hypothesis bounds it from above. In (b), as in Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest}, $\delta$ does not depend on $K$, the
indices, the $\sigma_j$ or $\rho_1$; in (a) the region, and with it $K$, is fixed before $\delta$. So (b) is not a case
of (a) as stated, and it is proved by the same argument, with Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest} in place of the tuple
estimate.

\begin{proof}
The proof is that of Lemma~\ref{lem:term-two}, with the tuple estimate in place of Lemma~\ref{lem:chains-two}; we give
what changes. Let $t_{\max} = \lceil (L + 1)/e_0 \rceil$. As there, the cofactor $n/r$ of an integer counted for a
tuple $\mathbf r$ is the product of a unique non-increasing tuple $y_1 \ge \dots \ge y_t \ge r_k$ of primes with $t \le
t_{\max}$. For each $t$ the pairs $(\mathbf r, \mathbf y)$ are the $(k + t)$-tuples of primes up
to $N_2$ that satisfy a list of at most $J_0 + t_{\max}^2 + t_{\max} + 2$ comparisons: the given ones, the order of
$\mathbf y$, $y_a \ge r_k$ and $N_1 < \prod \mathbf r \prod \mathbf y \le N_2$. Their constant terms are at most
$(\Lambda_1 + L + 1)\log q$. Every such tuple has its entries at least $q^{e_0}$, as $y_a \ge r_k$, its rest $(r_{i_1},
\dots, r_{i_K})$ in $\mathcal G_q$, and its product in $(N_1, N_2]$. Apply the tuple estimate with $k + t$ entries, $J_0
+ t_{\max}^2 + t_{\max} + 2$ comparisons and $\Lambda_1 + L + 1$, the rest at the indices $i_1, \dots, i_K$ of
$\mathbf r$; it gives $\delta_t$, and each $t$ contributes at most $q^{L - \delta_t}/\varphi(q)$. With $\delta_0 =
\min_{t \le
t_{\max}} \delta_t$, the sum over the $t_{\max} + 1$ values of $t$ is at most $(t_{\max} + 1) q^{L -
\delta_0}/\varphi(q) \le q^{L - \delta_0/2}/\varphi(q)$ for large $q$, and $\delta = \delta_0/2$ will do. For (b) the
same with Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest}, at $k + t$ entries, $J_0 + t_{\max}^2 + t_{\max} + 2$ comparisons,
$\Lambda_1 + L + 1$, $\rho_2$ and $\varepsilon$, in place of the tuple estimate; its hypotheses on the rest are those on
$\mathbf r$, since the rest's entries are entries of $\mathbf r$.
\end{proof}

When every counted integer's cofactor is one prime $y$, the cofactor can be in the rest too. This is what a box whose
cells leave room for exactly one more prime gives, and its cell is then known.

\begin{lemma}[the cofactor in the rest]\label{lem:term-rest-cof}
% Lean: (a) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_cofG; (b) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_cof; (c) SieveAd.cof_facts
Let $2 < L \le 5/2$, $e_0 > 0$, let $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$ be integers, and $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$ a real. In (a) and
(b), once $q$ is given, let $(r_1, \dots, r_k)$ run over the $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$ that satisfy a list of
at most $J_0$ comparisons with constant terms at most $\Lambda_1 \log q$, and let $r = r_1 \cdots r_k$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Let $K \ge 1$ and let $\mathcal G$ be a region of $(K + 1)$-tuples for which the tuple estimate holds at
  $(L, e_0)$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that for every $q \ge q_0$, every $a$ prime to $q$ and all
  distinct indices $i_1, \dots, i_K$ of $\{1, \dots, k\}$ the bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest}(a) holds if every
  tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing, has $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $i$, $r \le
  N_1$ and $N_2 < r r_k^2$, and every integer $y \ge r_k$ with $N_1 < ry \le N_2$ has $(r_{i_1}, \dots, r_{i_K}, y)
  \in \mathcal G_q$.
\item[(b)] Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}, and let $\rho_2 \ge 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$ with $L -
\rho_2 -
  \varepsilon > 0$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$
  prime to $q$. Let $K \ge 1$, let $i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\sigma_1,
  \dots, \sigma_{K + 1}$ and $\rho_1$ be reals such that $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant $(L - \rho_2 -
  \varepsilon; \sigma_1 - \varepsilon, \dots, \sigma_{K + 1} - \varepsilon)$, of $K + 1$ prime sums. The bound of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest}(a) holds if every tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing,
  has $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $i$, $r_{i_j} \ge q^{\sigma_j}$ for $j \le K$, $r \le N_1$ and $N_2 < r r_k^2$,
  and every integer $y \ge r_k$ with $N_1 < ry \le N_2$ has $y \ge q^{\sigma_{K + 1}}$ and $q^{\rho_1} \le y
  \prod_{j \le K} r_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}$.
\item[(c)] Let $x^{1/D} \ge 3$, and let $\mathfrak b$ be a box of $k$ cells with $l_i \le h_i$ for every $i$,
$h(\mathfrak
  b) + 2 \le D$ and $l(\mathfrak b) + 2 l_k \ge D + 1$. Let $p_1, \dots, p_k$ be integers with $x^{l_i/D} \le p_i <
  x^{h_i/D}$, and $r = p_1 \cdots p_k$. Then $r \le N_1$, $N_2 < r p_k^2$, and every integer $y \ge p_k$ with $N_1 < ry
  \le N_2$ has
  \[
    x^{\max(D - h(\mathfrak b) - 1,\, l_k)/D} \le y \le x^{(D - l(\mathfrak b) + 1)/D}.
  \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

In (a) and (b) the conditions $r \le N_1$ and $N_2 < r r_k^2$ make the cofactor one prime, and the rest has $K + 1$
members, the last the cofactor. Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest-cof}(c) gives those conditions for a box of the main tree,
and the cofactor's cell $(\max(D - h(\mathfrak b) - 1, l_k), D - l(\mathfrak b) + 1)$, its upper end included. The
main tree is the tree on the boxes of Definition~\ref{def:tree}, as against the trees over frozen parts of
Definition~\ref{def:frozen-tree}.

\begin{proof}
(a) and (b). As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest}, the integers counted for a tuple $\mathbf r$ are $n = r y_1
\cdots y_t$
with $y_1 \ge \dots \ge y_t \ge r_k$ primes. Only $t = 1$ remains: $t = 0$ gives $n = r \le N_1$, outside $(N_1, N_2]$,
and $t \ge 2$ gives $n \ge r r_k^2 > N_2$. The pairs $(\mathbf r, y)$ are the $(k + 1)$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$
that satisfy the given comparisons, $y \ge r_k$ and $N_1 < ry \le N_2$: at most $J_0 + 4$ comparisons, with constant
terms at most $(\Lambda_1 + L + 1)\log q$. Each has its entries at least $q^{e_0}$, and, by the hypothesis on $y$, its
rest $(r_{i_1}, \dots, r_{i_K}, y)$, at the indices $i_1, \dots, i_K, k + 1$, in $\mathcal G_q$ for (a), and with the
lower bounds $\sigma_j$ and the product in $[q^{\rho_1}, q^{\rho_2}]$ for (b). The tuple estimate with $k + 1$ entries,
$J_0 + 4$ comparisons and $\Lambda_1 + L + 1$, or Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest} with these and $\rho_2$,
$\varepsilon$, bounds the sum; $\delta$ is theirs.

(c). Since $x^{1/D} \ge 3$, $x^{(D - 1)/D} \le N_1$ and $N_2 < x^{(D + 1)/D}$; and $x^{l(\mathfrak b)/D} \le r \le
x^{h(\mathfrak b)/D}$. So $r \le x^{(D - 1)/D} \le N_1$, as $h(\mathfrak b) \le D - 2$; and $r p_k^2 \ge x^{(l(\mathfrak
b) + 2l_k)/D} \ge x^{(D + 1)/D} > N_2$. Let $y \ge p_k$ with $N_1 < ry \le N_2$. Then $y \ge p_k \ge x^{l_k/D}$; if $y
< x^{(D - h(\mathfrak b) - 1)/D}$, then $ry < x^{(D - 1)/D} \le N_1$, which is false; and if $y > x^{(D - l(\mathfrak b)
+ 1)/D}$, then $ry > x^{(D + 1)/D} > N_2$, which is false.
\end{proof}

Section~\ref{sec:window-tests} evaluates the terms of boxes with these lemmas: the test (W) through
Lemmas~\ref{lem:term-rest}(b) and~\ref{lem:term-rest-cof}(b), with $\operatorname{Res}_L$ on a quadrant, and the test
(W$'$) through Lemmas~\ref{lem:term-rest}(a) and~\ref{lem:term-rest-cof}(a), with the tuple estimate of
Lemma~\ref{lem:tied-to-tuples} for the box of the rest's cells.

\subsection{The two corners of a box}\label{sec:corners}

This subsection proves what the test (W$'$) of Section~\ref{sec:window-tests} needs: an inequality checked at two
corners of the box of the rest's cells gives the tuple estimate on that box. The condition on a quadrant asks for more
than this needs, so we tie the variable to the rest (Definition~\ref{def:res-tied}). The clamp gives one inequality
that implies the fit of Definition~\ref{def:fit} (Lemma~\ref{lem:clamp}); that inequality at two corners gives the
tied condition (Lemma~\ref{lem:two-corners}); and the tied condition gives the tuple estimate
(Lemma~\ref{lem:tied-to-tuples}).

A box of the decomposition gives each prime of the rest a cell, so each exponent $s_j$ an interval $[a_j, b_j]$; in
this subsection the product $\prod_j [a_j, b_j]$ of these intervals of reals is also called the box. The rest and the
variable make up $q^L$: on the box $s_1 + \dots + s_K + m$ is $L$ up to the dyadic blocks. The
condition on a quadrant (Definition~\ref{def:res-quadrant}) asks $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ at every
$s_j
\ge \sigma_j = a_j$ and every $m \ge m_0$, where $m_0$ is the exponent of the variable that the upper ends leave,
about $L - \sum_j
b_j$. It contains the corner $(m_0; a_1, \dots, a_K)$, where the prime sums are shortest and so is the variable, and
which no tuple of primes of the box reaches: there $\sum_j s_j + m$ falls short of $L$ by the box's width $\sum_j
(b_j - a_j)$. On a wide box the condition can fail at that corner and hold at every point that the box's tuples of
primes reach (a
heuristic, not proved). The condition below ties the variable to the rest.

\begin{definition}\label{def:res-tied}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResBoxT
Let $L$ and $w$ be real, $K \ge 1$, and $a_j, b_j$ real for $1 \le j \le K$. The condition $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds
\emph{on the box $\prod_j [a_j, b_j]$ with the variable tied to the rest}, with the margin $w$, if
$\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds for all reals $s_j \in [a_j, b_j]$ and $m$ with
\[
  L - w \le s_1 + \dots + s_K + m \le L + 10^{-5}.
\]
\end{definition}

The test (W$'$) of Definition~\ref{def:window-tests} gives this condition with the margin $w = 2 \cdot 10^{-5}$, on
the cells widened by $2 \cdot 10^{-5}$ at each end; Lemma~\ref{lem:tied-to-tuples} takes it in that form.

\emph{The clamp.} For an admissible tuple, write $Y = \sum_j s_j(t_j - 1)$, the exponent of $\prod_j Q_j^{t_j - 1}$, so
that the exponent of Definition~\ref{def:fit} is $E = Y + L - m$. The variable enters through the \emph{clamp}, which
replaces the two alternatives of Definition~\ref{def:fit} by one sufficient inequality (Lemma~\ref{lem:clamp}) whose
left side does not increase in $m$:
\begin{gather*}
  \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) = \min\Bigl(\frac{\max(1 - m, 0)}2,\ \max\Bigl(0, \frac\gamma2 + y_0(m)\Bigr)\Bigr), \\
  y_0(m) = \frac12\Bigl(\frac5{12} + \delta' + 2\varepsilon' - m\Bigl(\frac{11}{12} + \delta'\Bigr)\Bigr),
\end{gather*}
with $\delta' = \varepsilon' = 1/1000$, so $y_0(m) = (1259 - 2753m)/6000$: the first entry is orthogonality's, the
second the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality's. The clamp is non-negative, and it is $0$ for $m \ge 1$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:clamp}
% Lean: SieveResTest.binOK_of, SieveResTest.clampR, SieveResTest.y0R
Let $L$, $Y$, $\gamma$ and $m$ be real. If $Y + \gamma/2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) \le -1/25000$, then
the exponents $(Y + L - m, \gamma, m)$ fit at $L$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $E = Y + L - m$. If the clamp is its first entry, then, as $(m + \max(m, 1))/2 - m = \max(1 - m, 0)/2$,
$E + \gamma/2 + (m + \max(m, 1))/2 = L + Y + \gamma/2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) \le L - 1/25000$: the first
alternative of Definition~\ref{def:fit}. Otherwise the clamp is $\max(0, \gamma/2 + y_0(m))$, at least $0$ and at least
$\gamma/2 + y_0(m)$. As it is at least $0$, $E + \gamma/2 + m \le L - 1/25000$. Since $b(m) - m = 5/12 + \delta' +
2\varepsilon' - m(11/12 + \delta')$, $E + m/2 + \gamma + b(m)/2 = L + Y + \gamma/2 + (\gamma/2 + y_0(m))$, and as the
clamp is at least $\gamma/2 + y_0(m)$, this is at most $L - 1/25000$: the second alternative.
\end{proof}

\emph{The bound at a corner.} The test (W$'$) (Section~\ref{sec:window-tests}) checks the inequality of
Lemma~\ref{lem:clamp} at two corners of a box, in fixed-point arithmetic with the unit $2^{-64}$ (\S S7). There the
large values (Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers}) enter through a function $\operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma; e)$, which bounds $s(t -
1) - s\tau$ for a prime sum of exponent $s \ge e$ and count exponent $\gamma$ once $\gamma$ exceeds a threshold
$\Gamma_k$. Recall $\tau = 10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} = 10^{-5}$ (Section~\ref{sec:window-estimate}), and
let, for $k \ge 1$,
\begin{multline*}
  \operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma; e) = \max\Bigl(-e\Bigl(\frac3{10} - \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}\Bigr),
